- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-b). *Ammonium formate, LiChropur, eluent additive for LC-MS* (Product No. 70221) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sial/70221
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-c). *Glycoursodeoxycholic acid* (Product No. 06863) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/06863
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-d). *Methanol, suitable for HPLC, ≥99.9%* (Product No. 34860) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigald/34860
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-e). *Sodium tauroursodeoxycholate* (Product No. T0266) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/t0266
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-f). *Ursodeoxycholic acid* (Product No. U5127) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/u5127
- Want, E. J., Coen, M., Masson, P., Keun, H. C., Pearce, J. T. M., Reily, M. D., Robertson, D., Rohde, C. M., Holmes, E., Lindon, J., Plumb, R. S., McDonald, S., & Nicholson, J. K. (2011). *The application of UPLC-MSE for the analysis of bile acids in biological fluids* (Application Note No. 720004007). Waters Corporation. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.waters.com/nextgen/kr/ko/library/application-notes/2011/The-Application-of-UPLC-MSE-for-the-Analysis-of-Bile-Acids-in-Biological-Fluids.html

</ExpandableText>

### Discontinued Methods

<ExpandableText title="HALO Assay (failed protocol)" meta={<em>discontinued</em>}>

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/halo-assay-discontinued.pdf" workbookTab="HALO assay" />

<Callout title="This protocol failed">

This procedure did not work and was replaced by the liquid bile salt assay with
an LC-MS readout. It is published here because a method that fails is still a
result: the explanation of what went wrong belongs with the data analysis
section of the wiki, which this protocol should be linked to once that page
exists.

</Callout>

#### Introduction

The aim of the HALO assay is to investigate the activity of the 15 bile salt
hydrolases (BSH) that were transformed into *E. coli*. Liquid culture is
spotted onto agar medium supplemented with a defined concentration of a bile
salt. The activity of each BSH variant is analysed from the size of the halo
that forms around the spot as a result of the deconjugation reaction.

The assay comprises many conditions: 3 concentrations of bile salt, 16
transformed strains and the bile salts listed under Materials, without
replicates.

This procedure failed. The explanation of what went wrong is given in the data
analysis section of the wiki.

#### Materials

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Cryostocks of the transformed *E. coli* strains | 16 strains |
| LB broth with the appropriate antibiotic | 3–5 mL per strain |
| LB agar | Volume calculated for the number of plates (see the protocol "Preparing LB Broth and Agar Plates") |
| CaCl₂ stock, 100 mM | To a final concentration of 0.37 g/L in the agar |
| Distilled water | 1 mL per bile salt stock |
| Taurodeoxycholic acid sodium salt | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| GDCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| GCDCA sodium salt | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| GUDCA | Not soluble in distilled water |
| TLCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| TCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| TUDCA | Not heat resistant |
| TCDCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| GLCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| GCA | Amount calculated for the volume of medium poured |
| Ice and dry ice | For the bile salt stocks and the cryostocks |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| 6-well plates | 2 mL of medium per well |
| 96-well plate | For growth of many samples in the plate reader |
| Culture tubes | 1 per strain |
| Microcentrifuge tubes, 1.5 mL | 1 per bile salt stock |
| Sterile pipette tips or inoculation loops | For sampling the cryostocks |
| Flask | For the agar with CaCl₂ |
| Micropipettes and sterile tips | As required |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Shaking incubator | 37 °C, 200–250 rpm |
| Oven | 50–55 °C, for holding the molten agar |
| Vortex mixer | 1 |
| Spectrophotometer or plate reader | Measurement of the OD₆₀₀ |
| RESHAPE | For monitoring of halo growth |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |
| Insulated gloves | For handling dry ice |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

The SDS of each bile salt in use should be consulted individually; only two of
the bile salts are listed below.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Glycoursodeoxycholic acid (GUDCA) | 64480-66-6 | No hazard statements listed on the product page. | Avoid dust formation. The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-b) Product page |
| Sodium tauroursodeoxycholate (TUDCA) | 35807-85-3 | No hazard statements listed on the product page. | Avoid dust formation. The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-c) Product page |
| Calcium chloride | 10043-52-4 (anhydrous), 10035-04-8 (dihydrate) | Warning. H319: causes serious eye irritation. | Wear safety glasses and gloves. Dissolution of the anhydrous salt releases heat. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-a) SDS of the dihydrate |
| Dry ice (solid carbon dioxide) | 124-38-9 | Not classified under CLP. Cold burns (−78.5 °C). Asphyxiation risk in confined spaces. | Handle with insulated gloves. Use in a ventilated room. Never store in a sealed container. | Oy Linde Gas Ab (2023) |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

#### Procedure

**Planning the experiment**

1. Calculate the volume of medium and the amount of each bile salt required
   before the plates are poured.

<ProtocolNote>

The size of the halos is not known in advance. If resources allow, it is
recommended to divide the strains over two plates (8 strains each), so that the
halos do not overlap.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

Only 100 mg of some bile salts was available. In that case, all 16 strains may
have to be placed in one single-well OmniTray: 30 mL of medium for each
concentration requires 90 mg per bile salt.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 0: overnight culture**

2. Keep the glycerol cryostocks on dry ice or in the freezer as much as
   possible.
3. Scrape a small amount off the frozen surface with a sterile pipette tip or
   inoculation loop.
4. Inoculate directly into 3 to 5 mL of LB medium supplemented with the
   appropriate antibiotic.
5. Return the cryostock to the freezer.
6. Incubate overnight at 37 °C with shaking at 200 to 250 rpm.

**Day 0 or 1: LB agar plates with bile salt (6-well plate, 2 mL per well)**

7. Prepare the LB agar according to the protocol "Preparing LB Broth and Agar
   Plates", up to the step before the antibiotic is added, and keep it in the
   oven while it cools.
8. Calculate the volume of bile salt stock (100 mg/mL) required for each bile
   salt concentration (Table 2). For 0.5% (w/v; 5 mg/mL) in 4 mL of medium (2
   wells of 2 mL), 200 µL of stock is required.

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Bile salt stock volume (C1 × V1 = C2 × V2)."
  workbookTab="HALO assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "c1", label: "Bile salt stock concentration (C1)", value: 100, unit: "mg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "pFinal", label: "Final bile salt concentration", value: 0.5, unit: "% (w/v)", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Medium volume (V2)", value: 4, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "c2", label: "Final bile salt concentration (C2)", unit: "mg/mL", compute: (v) => v.pFinal * 10 },
    { key: "v1", label: "Stock volume to add (V1)", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => ((v.c2 * v.v2) / v.c1) * 1000 },
  ]}
/>

9. Check the heat resistance and the solubility of each bile salt.

<ProtocolNote>

GUDCA is not soluble in distilled water, and TUDCA is not heat resistant.

</ProtocolNote>

10. If the bile salt is soluble, dissolve it in 1 mL of distilled water in a
    microcentrifuge tube.
11. Vortex until the solution is clear.
12. Keep the stock on ice until use.
13. In a separate flask, supplement the cooled agar with CaCl₂ to a final
    concentration of 0.37 g/L, using the 100 mM stock (Table 3).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="CaCl₂ stock volume for the agar."
  workbookTab="HALO assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "gTarget", label: "Target CaCl₂ concentration", value: 0.37, unit: "g/L", input: true },
    { key: "molarMass", label: "Molar mass of CaCl₂ (anhydrous)", value: 110.98, unit: "g/mol", input: true },
    { key: "cStock", label: "CaCl₂ stock concentration", value: 100, unit: "mM", input: true },
    { key: "vAgar", label: "Agar volume", value: 100, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "cTarget", label: "Target CaCl₂ concentration", unit: "mM", compute: (v) => (v.gTarget / v.molarMass) * 1000 },
    { key: "vStock", label: "CaCl₂ stock to add", unit: "mL", compute: (v) => (v.cTarget * v.vAgar) / v.cStock },
  ]}
/>

14. Add the required volume of bile salt stock to each well first.
15. Pour the agar with CaCl₂ into the wells, one plate at a time.
16. Move the plate from left to right to spread the mixture.

<ProtocolNote>

If time allows before the agar sets, the mixture can be mixed with a pipette
tip.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: liquid cultures**

17. Dilute the overnight cultures 1:50 and grow them to an OD₆₀₀ of 0.5 to 0.6.

<ProtocolNote>

For a large number of samples, the cultures can be grown in a 96-well plate in
a plate reader (120 µL per well).

</ProtocolNote>

18. Dilute the cultures to the same OD₆₀₀ (Table 4).

<ProtocolNote>

The cultures will probably all have a different OD₆₀₀.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={4}
  title="Normalization of the OD₆₀₀ (OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2)."
  workbookTab="HALO assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "od1", label: "Measured OD₆₀₀ of the culture (OD1)", value: 0.8, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "od2", label: "Target OD₆₀₀ (OD2)", value: 0.5, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Final volume (V2)", value: 120, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "v1", label: "Culture to add (V1)", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.od2 * v.v2) / v.od1 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.v2 - v.v1 },
  ]}
/>

**Day 1: spotting and monitoring**

19. Pipette 5 µL of each liquid culture onto a plate, leaving enough distance
    between the spots for the halos to grow.
20. Insert the plates into the RESHAPE.
21. Monitor the halo growth for 48 to 72 hours.

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Volume of bile salt stock**

*c*2 = *p* × 10  and  V1 = (*c*2 × V2) / *c*1

in which:

- *p* = required bile salt concentration (% w/v)
- *c*2 = required concentration (mg/mL)
- *c*1 = concentration of the stock (mg/mL)
- V1 = volume of stock to add
- V2 = volume of medium

A concentration of 1% (w/v) is 1 g per 100 mL, which equals 10 mg/mL; this is
the origin of the factor 10.

**Worked example.** For 0.5% (5 mg/mL) in 4 mL of medium from a 100 mg/mL
stock: V1 = (5 × 4) / 100 = 0.2 mL = 200 µL.

**Volume of CaCl₂ stock for the agar**

*c* = (*g* / *M*) × 1000  and  V1 = (*c* × V2) / *c*1

in which:

- *g* = required concentration (g/L)
- *M* = molar mass of CaCl₂ (g/mol)
- *c* = required concentration (mM)
- *c*1 = concentration of the stock (mM)
- V1 = volume of stock to add
- V2 = volume of agar

**Worked example.** For 0.37 g/L CaCl₂ (*M* = 110.98 g/mol) in 100 mL of agar
from a 100 mM stock: *c* = (0.37 / 110.98) × 1000 = 3.33 mM, and V1 =
(3.33 × 100) / 100 = 3.33 mL.

**Normalization of the optical density**

OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2 → V1 = (OD2 × V2) / OD1

in which:

- OD1 = measured optical density of the culture
- V1 = volume of culture to use
- OD2 = required optical density
- V2 = final volume after dilution

The volume of medium to add is V2 − V1. The relation holds because the number
of cells is conserved on dilution.

**Worked example.** To obtain 120 µL at an OD₆₀₀ of 0.5 from a culture at
OD₆₀₀ 0.8: V1 = (0.5 × 120) / 0.8 = 75.0 µL of culture and 120 − 75 = 45.0 µL
of medium.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- Oy Linde Gas Ab. (2023). *Carbon dioxide, solid (dry ice)* [Safety data sheet]. https://static.prd.echannel.linde.com/wcsstore/FI_REN_Industrial_Gas_Store/pdf/SDS_EN/Carbon%20dioxide_solid_Dry%20ice_.pdf
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-a). *Calcium chloride dihydrate* (Product No. C3306) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/c3306
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-b). *Glycoursodeoxycholic acid* (Product No. 06863) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/06863
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-c). *Sodium tauroursodeoxycholate* (Product No. T0266) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/t0266

</ExpandableText>

## C. Toolkit Development

Characterizing *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 as a chassis, assembling the promoter
library, transferring it into the chassis by conjugation, and characterizing
the reporters. Protocols shared with the BSH workstream — the minipreps, the
quantification, the backbone PCR, the gel, the colony PCR, the clean-up and the
cryostock — are listed under Section B and are used unchanged here.

### Chassis Characterization

<ExpandableText title="Medium Comparison Growth Assay">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/medium-comparison-growth-assay-2.pdf" workbookTab="Medium comparison" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes a growth assay that compares the anaerobic minimal
medium for *Phocaeicola vulgatus* with the rich medium mGAM for *P. vulgatus*
DSM1447, and tests which components of mGAM account for the difference. Starch,
vitamin K1 and pH are added to the minimal medium individually and in
combination, each at the concentration found in mGAM, and growth is followed as
the optical density in a microtiter plate.

The aim is to find the simplest medium that still supports growth comparable to
mGAM, so that experiments which need a defined medium are not limited by poor
growth.

#### Materials

**Strains**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 | Cryostock |

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Minimal medium for *P. vulgatus* | The anaerobic medium with hemin; see the protocol "Preparation of Minimal Medium for *P. vulgatus*" |
| mGAM broth and mGAM agar plates | See the protocol "Preparation of mGAM Broth and Agar Plates" |
| Soluble starch, autoclavable | For a 5% (w/v) stock in distilled water |
| Vitamin K1 (phytomenadione) | For a 1000 µg/mL stock in absolute ethanol; density 0.984 g/mL |
| Ethanol, absolute | For the vitamin K1 stock |
| Sodium hydroxide solution | For adjusting the pH |
| Distilled water, sterile | For the border wells |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| 96-well microtiter plate | One per experiment |
| Sticky plate seals, transparent, single-peel | One per plate; seals rather than lids |
| Microcentrifuge tubes, 1.5 mL | One per medium condition |
| Conical tubes, 15 mL | For transferring medium out of the chamber |
| Syringe filters, 0.22 µm, with syringes | For the vitamin K1 stock |
| Inoculation loops, micropipettes and sterile tips | As required |
| Insulated container | For transfer between chambers |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Anaerobic chamber with incubator | 37 °C |
| Microplate reader with temperature control and shaking | 37 °C, absorbance at 600 nm |
| pH meter | For adjusting the minimal medium to pH 7.3 |
| Magnetic stirrer with heating | For the starch stock |
| Autoclave | For the starch stock |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

Sodium hydroxide is corrosive. Ethanol is flammable and is handled away from
ignition sources.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sodium hydroxide solution | 1310-73-2 | Danger. H314: causes severe skin burns and eye damage. | Wear gloves and eye protection. | Supplier SDS |
| Vitamin K1 (phytomenadione) | 84-80-0 | The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Light sensitive; keep the stock dark. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-b) Product page |
| Ethanol, absolute | 64-17-5 | Danger. H225: highly flammable liquid and vapour. H319: causes serious eye irritation. | Keep away from ignition sources. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-a) |
| Soluble starch | 9005-84-9 | Not classified as hazardous. | Standard laboratory practice. | Supplier SDS |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

#### Procedure

**Preparing the stocks**

1. Prepare the starch stock at 5% (w/v): weigh 1 g of soluble starch, combine
   it with 2 mL of distilled water while stirring to form a slurry, then add
   the remaining 18 mL while stirring and heating, and autoclave the solution.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

Starch does not dissolve if the full volume of water is added at once. The
slurry is made first, which is what allows the rest of the water to be stirred
in.

</ProtocolNote>

2. Prepare the vitamin K1 stock at 1000 µg/mL in absolute ethanol, which is
   1 µL of vitamin K1 per mL of ethanol given its density of 0.984 g/mL, and
   filter-sterilize it.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

Vitamin K1 is light sensitive; the stock is kept dark.

</ProtocolNote>

**From cryostock to liquid culture**

3. Collect the cryostock from the −80 °C freezer immediately before the
   session, in an insulated box with ice, and take it into the chamber with the
   rest of the material.
4. Scrape the frozen surface with an inoculation loop and streak an mGAM agar
   plate for single colonies, then return the cryostock to the freezer
   immediately.
5. Incubate for two days at 37 °C and check the plate for purity: uniform
   morphology and colour, and no contaminants.
6. Inoculate two or three colonies into approximately 5 mL of minimal medium
   and the same into mGAM, and grow overnight.

<ProtocolNote>

The plate is kept in case contamination appears later in the experiment.

</ProtocolNote>

**Preparing the medium conditions**

7. Take the volume of minimal medium needed out of the chamber in a conical
   tube for the pH adjustment.
8. Measure the starting pH, then raise it to 7.3 with sodium hydroxide, which
   is the pH of mGAM.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The pH is measured on a small aliquot, not with the probe in the bottle of
medium.

</ProtocolNote>

9. Prepare the supplemented batches by adding the stock volumes per mL of
   medium given in Table 2.

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Supplement stock volumes per mL of medium."
  workbookTab="Medium comparison"
  rows={[
    { key: "cStarchStock", label: "Starch stock concentration", value: 5, unit: "%", input: true },
    { key: "cStarchFinal", label: "Starch final concentration", value: 5, unit: "g/L", input: true },
    { key: "vStarch", label: "Starch stock per mL of medium", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.cStarchFinal / (v.cStarchStock * 10)) * 1000 },
    { key: "cVitKStock", label: "Vitamin K1 stock concentration", value: 1000, unit: "µg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "cVitKFinal", label: "Vitamin K1 final concentration", value: 5, unit: "µg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "vVitK", label: "Vitamin K1 stock per mL of medium", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.cVitKFinal / v.cVitKStock) * 1000 },
  ]}
/>

<ProtocolNote>

The supplement concentrations match those of mGAM: 5 g/L starch and 5 µg/mL
vitamin K1. Combinations are made by adding the relevant stocks to the same
batch, and any condition that includes pH is made from the pH-adjusted batch.

</ProtocolNote>

10. Prepare the eight conditions: minimal medium alone, with starch, with
    vitamin K1, at pH 7.3, and the combinations of these, together with mGAM as
    the reference.

**Normalization and plate layout**

11. Measure the OD₆₀₀ of the overnight cultures, using the corresponding
    uninoculated medium as the blank.
12. Normalize the cultures to the lowest OD₆₀₀ obtained (Table 3).

<ProtocolNote>

Normalizing to the lowest value means every culture is diluted rather than
concentrated.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="Normalization of the overnight cultures."
  workbookTab="Medium comparison"
  rows={[
    { key: "od1", label: "Measured OD₆₀₀", value: 0.9, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "od2", label: "Target OD₆₀₀ (the lowest obtained)", value: 0.4, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Final volume", value: 2, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "v1", label: "Culture to use", unit: "mL", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => (v.od2 * v.v2) / v.od1 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium to add", unit: "mL", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => v.v2 - v.v1 },
    { key: "dilution", label: "Dilution into the wells (1 in …)", value: 1000, unit: "fold", input: true },
    { key: "vPerMl", label: "Normalized culture per mL of medium in the well", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => 1000 / v.dilution },
  ]}
/>

13. Pipette 100 µL of each medium condition into its wells of a 96-well plate,
    in triplicate.
14. Pipette 100 µL of sterile distilled water into all the border wells.

<ProtocolNote>

The water border reduces the evaporation that otherwise affects the outer wells
first.

</ProtocolNote>

15. Include uninoculated wells of each medium as the blank, and wells of mGAM
    with and without cells as the reference.
16. Add the normalized culture last, at a 1:1000 dilution, immediately before
    sealing.

<ProtocolNote>

Adding the inoculum last means growth starts at the same moment in every well.

</ProtocolNote>

17. Seal the plate with a transparent sticky seal.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

A lid is not used: it allows evaporation over the course of a long measurement,
which distorts the optical density.

</ProtocolNote>

**Measurement**

18. Transfer the plate to the reader in a closed insulated container.
19. Set the reader to 37 °C, absorbance at 600 nm, with continuous orbital
    shaking and a reading every 30 minutes.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The plate is shaken immediately before every reading, because *P. vulgatus*
sediments and a settled well reads far below its true density.

</ProtocolNote>

20. Run the measurement for the intended duration and export the readings for
    the growth curves.

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="July 2026">

An earlier version of this assay went from cryostock directly to liquid
culture. Going from cryostock to a plate and then to liquid culture is
preferred, because it gives single colonies and allows the purity of the
culture to be checked.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The comparison tests starch, vitamin K1 and pH because these are the components
of mGAM that the minimal medium for *P. vulgatus* lacks or differs in. Hemin is
in both media and is therefore not a variable here.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The medium used in this assay is the anaerobic minimal medium for
*P. vulgatus*. The aerobic minimal medium of the liquid bile salt assay is for
*E. coli*, contains no hemin, and is not used here.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Supplement volumes per millilitre of medium**

*V* = *c*final / *c*stock

in which:

- *V* = volume of stock per mL of medium (µL)
- *c*final = required final concentration
- *c*stock = concentration of the stock, in the same unit

A percentage w/v is grams per 100 mL, so a 5% starch stock is 50 g/L. The
supplement concentrations are chosen to match those of mGAM, so that the effect
of each component can be tested one at a time.

**Worked example.** For 5 g/L starch from a 5% (50 g/L) stock: 5 / 50 = 0.1 mL
= 100 µL per mL of medium. For 5 µg/mL vitamin K1 from a 1000 µg/mL stock:
5.0 µL per mL of medium.

**Normalization of the overnight cultures**

OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2

in which:

- OD1 = measured OD₆₀₀ of the culture
- V1 = volume of culture to use
- OD2 = target OD₆₀₀, taken as the lowest obtained across the cultures
- V2 = final volume

Normalizing to the lowest optical density obtained means every culture can be
diluted rather than concentrated. The normalized culture is then diluted into
the wells so that growth starts fresh in each.

**Worked example.** To reach OD₆₀₀ 0.4 in 2 mL from a culture at OD₆₀₀ 0.9: V1
= (0.4 × 2) / 0.9 = 0.889 mL of culture and 1.111 mL of medium. A 1:1000
dilution into the wells is 1.0 µL of normalized culture per mL of medium.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-a). *Ethanol absolute for analysis EMSURE* (Product No. 1.00983) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/mm/100983
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-b). *Vitamin K1* (Product No. V3501) [Product information]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/product/sigma/v3501

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Agar Well-Diffusion Susceptibility Assay" meta={<em>not performed</em>}>

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/agar-well-diffusion-susceptibility-assay-not-performed.pdf" />

<Callout title="This assay was not performed">

It is published as an alternative to the broth microdilution MIC assay that the
team wrote up but did not run, for following inhibition kinetics by time-lapse
imaging rather than reading a single endpoint. It is recorded here so that it
can be picked up if the work is continued.

</Callout>

#### Introduction

This protocol describes an agar well-diffusion susceptibility assay. Instead of
placing an antibiotic-impregnated paper disk on the agar surface, as in the
classic disk diffusion assay, a well is punched into the agar and filled with a
defined volume of antibiotic solution. This gives a diffusion source of known
concentration and volume, which makes the assay suited to time-lapse imaging of
the inhibition zone as it forms.

Conventional disk diffusion is read once, after 18 to 24 hours. This approach
allows the kinetics of diffusion and growth inhibition to be followed from the
start of the experiment.

This assay was not performed. It is recorded here so that it can be picked up
if the work is continued.

#### Materials

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Mueller-Hinton agar, or the medium validated for the organism | Plates poured to a constant thickness of approximately 4 mm |
| Antibiotic stocks and dilutions | See the loading concentrations established during optimization |
| Solvent used for the antibiotic stocks | For the negative control well |
| Reference strains | One susceptible and one resistant strain with a known profile |
| McFarland standard, 0.5 | For standardizing the inoculum |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Well punch (cork borer, 6 mm), or a cut sterile pipette tip or straw of fixed diameter | The same punch for a whole series |
| Sterile needle or pipette tip | For removing the agar plug |
| Sterile cotton swabs or an L-spreader | For plating the lawn |
| Micropipette, 10–200 µL, with sterile filter tips | For loading the wells |
| Petri dishes | Of consistent size |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Time-lapse imaging and incubation system | For following the inhibition zone |
| Incubator at the temperature appropriate to the organism | With an anaerobic jar or cabinet where required |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

The antibiotics and solvents are those of the susceptibility work; their
hazards are listed in the protocol "Minimum Inhibitory Concentration Testing".

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Antibiotic stock solutions and their solvents | Mixtures | See the safety table of the protocol "Minimum Inhibitory Concentration Testing". | Weigh powders in a fume hood; wear gloves and safety glasses. | Supplier SDS |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

#### Procedure

**Preparing the plates and the inoculum**

1. Pour the agar plates to a constant, predefined thickness of approximately
   4 mm.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The thickness must not vary between plates within one series: it changes the
shape of the diffusion front and therefore the size of the zone.

</ProtocolNote>

2. Standardize the inoculum to a 0.5 McFarland standard from a fresh colony or
   broth culture.
3. Plate the inoculum as a confluent lawn with a swab or L-spreader, passing in
   three directions and rotating the plate 60 degrees between passes.
4. Let the surface dry for 3 to 5 minutes with the lid closed at room
   temperature before punching.

**Punching and loading the wells**

5. Punch wells of a fixed diameter at pre-marked, symmetrical positions, three
   or four per plate, equidistant from each other and from the edge.
6. Remove the agar plug with a sterile needle or pipette tip without damaging
   the bottom of the well.
7. Use the same punch, or punches of identical diameter, for every plate in a
   series.
8. Pipette a fixed volume of the antibiotic solution into each well.

<ProtocolNote>

A volume of 20 µL is a reasonable starting point; whichever value is chosen is
kept the same across the series, because variation translates directly into the
derived diffusion coefficient.

</ProtocolNote>

9. Include a well loaded with solvent alone as a negative control on every
   plate.
10. Decide whether imaging starts immediately as the zero time point, or after
    a pre-diffusion of 15 to 30 minutes at room temperature with the lid
    closed, and apply the same choice throughout.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

This choice defines the zero point of the kinetics and must be fixed for the
whole series.

</ProtocolNote>

**Incubation and imaging**

11. Place the plates inverted in the imaging and incubation system at the
    temperature appropriate to the organism.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

For metronidazole against anaerobic or micro-aerophilic strains, incubate
anaerobically: metronidazole is metabolically inactive under aerobic
conditions, whatever the intrinsic susceptibility of the strain.

</ProtocolNote>

12. Capture the inhibition zone frame by frame from the zero time point for at
    least 24 hours, and longer for slow-growing organisms.
13. Confirm with the documentation of the imaging system whether its
    zone-detection works on round wells as it does on disks.

**Quality control**

14. Keep the well diameter and the agar depth identical across all plates in a
    series.
15. Keep the pipetted volume and the concentration exact and consistent.
16. Keep the inoculum density and the drying time before punching the same.
17. Include a susceptible and a resistant reference strain as controls in every
    series.

<ProtocolNote>

The kinetic parameters are derived directly from the imaging data rather than
read against a published breakpoint table, so consistency between wells and
plates matters more here than in a conventional endpoint reading.

</ProtocolNote>

**Optimizing the loading dose**

18. For each antibiotic, test a two- to three-fold dilution series around the
    intended loading concentration on a reference strain of known phenotype.
19. Choose the concentration that gives a clear zone-growth curve without the
    zone reaching the edge of the plate within the measurement period.
20. Record the chosen concentration for each antibiotic and use it for every
    experiment in the series.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

Loading concentrations derived from standard disk potencies are a starting
point only. They are not validated for a particular organism, medium or well
geometry and have to be optimized before the assay is used.

</ProtocolNote>

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

This assay was not performed. It was written as an alternative to the broth
microdilution MIC assay, for following inhibition kinetics by time-lapse
imaging rather than reading a single endpoint.

</ProtocolNote>

<Callout title="Still to be added">

The draft referred to a table of loading concentrations that was not included
in the file. It needs to be added, or the reference to it removed.

</Callout>

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Minimum Inhibitory Concentration Testing">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/minimum-inhibitory-concentration-testing-2.pdf" workbookTab="MIC assay" />

#### Introduction

The minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC) is the lowest concentration of an
antibiotic that completely inhibits the visible growth of an organism. It is
measured here by broth microdilution in a 96-well microtiter plate: a two-fold
dilution series of each antibiotic is prepared in medium, every well is
inoculated with the same amount of culture, and growth is read as the optical
density after incubation.

The assay serves two purposes in this project: to establish which antibiotics
*Phocaeicola vulgatus* DSM1447 is susceptible to, and to confirm which are
usable as selection markers because the organism is resistant to them.

The working concentrations of the two-fold dilution series are taken from the
literature for each antibiotic. The antibiotics are diluted in mGAM, and the
assay is carried out anaerobically and in triplicate. A run is repeated if the
minimum inhibitory concentration falls outside the concentration range tested,
or if the replicates do not agree.

Two values are read from the same plate. The minimum inhibitory concentration
of a repeat is the lowest concentration at which no growth is visible. Alongside
it the IC₉₀, the lowest concentration giving 90% inhibition of growth, is
determined from the optical density, because it yields a quantitative value
rather than a visual call (Veiga et al., 2019).

#### Attributions

The broth microdilution format was recommended by Ruben Vazquez-Uribe,
principal investigator of the iGEM KU Leuven 2026 team.

#### Materials

**Strains**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447, and any further strains to be tested | 15 mL of overnight culture per strain |

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| mGAM broth | For the dilution series and the cultures |
| Antibiotic stock solutions | See Table 2 for the stocks and ranges used |
| Solvents for the stocks: ethanol, DMSO, distilled water | A small working aliquot is taken into the chamber, not the bottle |
| Distilled water, sterile | For the solvent control series |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| 96-well microtiter plates | One per group of antibiotics, plus one master plate |
| Plate replicators (stamps) | One set per antibiotic plate, to avoid carry-over |
| Plate lids | One per plate |
| Multichannel pipette, micropipettes and sterile tips | As required |
| Reservoirs | For the multichannel pipette |
| Microcentrifuge tubes, 1.5 mL | For the working solutions |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Anaerobic chamber with incubator | 37 °C |
| Microplate reader | OD₆₀₀ |
| Autoclave | For sterilizing the replicators between uses |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

Several of the antibiotics tested are classified for reproductive toxicity or
carcinogenicity. Powders are weighed in a fume hood and stock solutions are
prepared there. Persons who are pregnant or have a penicillin allergy should
check the hazard statements before handling the corresponding compounds.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ampicillin sodium salt | 69-52-3 | Danger. H317: may cause an allergic skin reaction. H334: may cause allergy or asthma symptoms if inhaled. | Avoid dust formation and inhalation. Not to be handled by persons with a penicillin allergy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-a) |
| Chloramphenicol | 56-75-7 | Danger. Suspected of causing cancer; the SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Weigh in a fume hood. Wear gloves and safety glasses. | Supplier SDS |
| Erythromycin | 114-07-8 | The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Avoid dust formation. Stock solutions are prepared in ethanol in a fume hood. | Supplier SDS |
| Metronidazole | 443-48-1 | Danger. Suspected of causing cancer and of damaging fertility; the SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Weigh in a fume hood. Wear gloves and safety glasses. | Supplier SDS |
| Tetracycline | 60-54-8 | Danger. Suspected of damaging the unborn child; the SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Avoid dust formation. Not to be handled during pregnancy. | Supplier SDS |
| Gentamicin sulfate | 1405-41-0 | Danger. The SDS of the product in use should be consulted for the hazard statements. | Avoid dust formation and inhalation. Wear gloves and safety glasses. | Supplier SDS |
| Dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO) | 67-68-5 | H227: combustible liquid. Rapidly absorbed through the skin. | Wear nitrile gloves. Dissolved substances can be carried through the skin. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-b) |
| Ethanol, 70% (v/v) | 64-17-5 | Danger. H225: highly flammable liquid and vapour. H319: causes serious eye irritation. | Keep away from open flames and hot surfaces. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-c) SDS of absolute ethanol |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

<Callout title="Confirm before repeating">

The incubation temperature of 30 °C and the reading wavelength of 630 nm come
from the reported runs and differ from the 37 °C and 600 nm used elsewhere in
the project. Both should be confirmed before the assay is repeated. The CAS
numbers and hazard statements of the antibiotics other than ampicillin also
still need to be confirmed against the SDS of the products in use, and the full
reference for Veiga et al. (2019) supplied.

</Callout>

#### Procedure

**Day 0: overnight culture**

1. Prepare 15 mL of overnight culture of the strain in mGAM broth, enough to
   fill the master plate.

**Preparing the antibiotic working solutions**

2. Establish the concentration range for each antibiotic from the literature
   and from the expected susceptibility (Table 2).

**Table 2.** Antibiotic stocks, solvents and concentration ranges used.

| Antibiotic | Stock | Solvent | Highest | Lowest | Storage |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ampicillin | 100 mg/mL | 50% ethanol | 32 µg/mL | 0.5 µg/mL | −20 °C |
| Chloramphenicol | 34 mg/mL | 95% ethanol | 16 µg/mL | 0.25 µg/mL | −20 °C |
| Erythromycin | 10 mg/mL | 100% ethanol | 32 µg/mL | 0.5 µg/mL | −20 °C |
| Metronidazole | 10 mg/mL | 100% DMSO | 32 µg/mL | 0.5 µg/mL | −20 °C |
| Tetracycline | 2.5 mg/mL | 50% ethanol | 16 µg/mL | 0.25 µg/mL | −20 °C |
| Gentamicin | 200 mg/mL | Distilled water | 1024 µg/mL | 16 µg/mL | −20 °C |

<ProtocolNote>

A two-fold dilution series is the standard format in the susceptibility
literature. The range for gentamicin is far higher than for the others, because
the organism is expected to be resistant and the series has to extend past the
point of inhibition to show that.

</ProtocolNote>

3. Prepare a working solution of each antibiotic in mGAM at the highest
   concentration of its series (Table 3).

<ProtocolNote>

A stock that is far more concentrated than the top of the series is pre-diluted
first, so that the volume to pipette stays accurate.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="Antibiotic working solution (C1 × V1 = C2 × V2)."
  workbookTab="MIC assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "c1", label: "Antibiotic stock concentration (C1)", value: 10000, unit: "µg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "c2", label: "Highest concentration in the series (C2)", value: 32, unit: "µg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "vWell", label: "Volume per well", value: 360, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "rows", label: "Replicate rows per antibiotic", value: 3, unit: "rows", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Working solution to prepare (V2)", value: 1500, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "v1", label: "Stock to add (V1)", unit: "µL", compute: (v) => (v.c2 * v.v2) / v.c1 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 2, compute: (v) => v.v2 - v.v1 },
    { key: "vNeeded", label: "Volume actually needed for the replicates", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.vWell * v.rows },
  ]}
/>

**Preparing the dilution plates**

4. Pipette the volume of medium that each well needs into every well of the
   dilution series, leaving the first row for the working solution.
5. Add the working solution to the first row and carry out the two-fold series
   down the plate, transferring an equal volume into an equal volume of medium
   at each step and mixing before each transfer (Table 4).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={4}
  title="Two-fold dilution series."
  workbookTab="MIC assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "cTop", label: "Highest concentration", value: 32, unit: "µg/mL", input: true },
    { key: "steps", label: "Number of dilution steps", value: 7, unit: "steps", input: true },
    { key: "vTransfer", label: "Volume transferred per step", value: 180, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "vWell", label: "Medium already in each well", value: 180, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "factor", label: "Dilution factor per step", unit: "–", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.vTransfer + v.vWell) / v.vTransfer },
    { key: "cLowest", label: "Lowest concentration in the series", unit: "µg/mL", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => v.cTop / Math.pow(v.factor, v.steps - 1) },
  ]}
/>

6. Leave the last row as inoculated mGAM without antibiotic. This is the
   positive control.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

Without a positive control the assay cannot be interpreted: a plate in which
nothing grows is indistinguishable from a plate in which the inoculum failed.
The positive control is also the denominator of the inhibition calculation, so
every plate needs its own.

</ProtocolNote>

7. Prepare a parallel series of the solvent alone, at the concentrations the
   solvent reaches in the antibiotic series, inoculated in the same way. This
   is the blank.

<ProtocolNote>

The blank is the inoculated antibiotic solvent, for example 50% ethanol for
ampicillin. It shows whether inhibition comes from the antibiotic or from the
ethanol or DMSO it is dissolved in, and it is the value subtracted in the
inhibition calculation.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

Only a small working aliquot of ethanol or DMSO is taken into the anaerobic
chamber, in a closed tube, rather than the bottle itself. The volumes used are
small enough that the solvents do not need to be equilibrated in the chamber
the day before.

</ProtocolNote>

**Inoculation**

8. Pipette 150 µL of the overnight culture into each well of a separate 96-well
   plate, the master plate.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The orientation of the master plate is kept the same every time it is used, so
that each pin of the replicator always corresponds to the same strain. This
matters even when only one strain is tested.

</ProtocolNote>

9. Load the master plate immediately before stamping.

<ProtocolNote>

The density of the inoculum starts to drift as soon as the plate leaves the
chamber, so the stamping follows without delay.

</ProtocolNote>

10. Dip the pins of the replicator into the master plate and stamp them into
    every antibiotic-containing well and into the growth control well of a
    dilution plate, in one movement, keeping the orientation the same.
11. Use a separate replicator for each antibiotic plate.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

A replicator used on more than one antibiotic plate carries antibiotic and
cells from one plate to the next. The replicators are not discarded; they are
sterilized and reused.

</ProtocolNote>

**Incubation and reading**

12. Cover each plate with a lid and incubate anaerobically for 48 hours at
    30 °C.

<ProtocolNote>

A lid rather than a seal is used here, because the plates are read and returned
to the incubator rather than sealed for a continuous measurement.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The incubation temperature of 30 °C is the value recorded for the runs
reported. It is lower than the 37 °C at which *P. vulgatus* is routinely grown,
and it should be confirmed before the assay is repeated, because the
temperature affects both the growth rate and the apparent susceptibility.

</ProtocolNote>

13. Read the OD₆₃₀ of every well after the 48 hours.

**Determining the MIC and the IC₉₀**

14. Record the MIC of each repeat as the lowest antibiotic concentration at
    which no growth is visible, provided that the positive control grew and the
    blank did not inhibit.
15. Calculate the percentage of inhibition of every well from its optical
    density, the blank and the positive control (Table 5).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={5}
  title="Percentage of inhibition and IC₉₀."
  workbookTab="MIC assay"
  rows={[
    { key: "odWell", label: "OD₆₃₀ of the well", value: 0.182, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "odBlank", label: "OD₆₃₀ of the blank (inoculated antibiotic solvent)", value: 0.062, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "odPositive", label: "OD₆₃₀ of the positive control (inoculated mGAM)", value: 0.742, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "inhibition", label: "Percentage of inhibition", unit: "%", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (1 - (v.odWell - v.odBlank) / (v.odPositive - v.odBlank)) * 100 },
    { key: "threshold", label: "Threshold for the IC₉₀", value: 90, unit: "%", input: true },
    { key: "reaches", label: "Does this well reach the threshold?", unit: "–", computeText: (v) => (v.inhibition >= v.threshold ? "Yes — at or above the IC₉₀" : "No — below the IC₉₀") },
  ]}
/>

16. Read the IC₉₀ of each repeat as the lowest concentration reaching 90%
    inhibition.
17. Take the IC₉₀ of a strain for an antibiotic as the consensus of the repeats.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

When two concentrations are supported by the same number of repeats, for
example three repeats indicating 2 µg/mL and three indicating 4 µg/mL, the
higher value is taken as the IC₉₀.

</ProtocolNote>

18. Repeat the assay if the MIC falls outside the concentration range tested,
    or if the replicates do not give a consensus.

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

The assay was run on 4 August 2026 on *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 and on a second
isolate, with the concentration ranges lowered relative to an earlier attempt.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

Gentamicin, kanamycin and spectinomycin were expected to allow growth at every
concentration tested, which confirms the intrinsic resistance that makes them
usable as selection markers rather than as inhibitors.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

Three replicate rows per antibiotic at 360 µL per well need 1080 µL, so
1500 µL of working solution was prepared per antibiotic.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The antibiotic list in the team notes marks imipenem and metronidazole as
having had barely any effect in an earlier agar dilution experiment.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Antibiotic working solution**

C1 × V1 = C2 × V2 → V1 = (C2 × V2) / C1

in which:

- C1 = concentration of the antibiotic stock
- V1 = volume of stock to add
- C2 = highest concentration of the dilution series
- V2 = volume of working solution to prepare

The volume to prepare follows from the volume per well and the number of
replicate rows, with an excess for pipetting loss. Stocks that are far more
concentrated than the top of the series are pre-diluted, so that the volume to
pipette stays accurate.

**Worked example.** For a top concentration of 32 µg/mL in 1500 µL from a
10,000 µg/mL stock: V1 = (32 × 1500) / 10,000 = 4.80 µL of stock and
1495.20 µL of medium. Three replicate rows at 360 µL need 1080 µL, so 1500 µL
is sufficient.

**Two-fold dilution series**

*f* = (*V*transfer + *V*well) / *V*transfer  and  *c*lowest = *c*top / *f*^(*n*−1)

in which:

- *f* = dilution factor per step
- *V*transfer = volume transferred into the next well (µL)
- *V*well = volume of medium already in that well (µL)
- *c*top = highest concentration of the series
- *n* = number of concentrations in the series
- *c*lowest = lowest concentration

Transferring an equal volume into an equal volume of medium halves the
concentration at every step, which is the two-fold series used in
susceptibility testing. The last row of the plate holds medium without
antibiotic as the growth control.

**Worked example.** Transferring 180 µL into 180 µL gives *f* = 2.0. Starting
at 32 µg/mL over 7 concentrations the lowest is 32 / 2⁶ = 0.500 µg/mL.

**Percentage of inhibition**

% inhibition = (1 − (OD_well − OD_blank) / (OD_positive control − OD_blank)) × 100

in which:

- OD_well = optical density of the well containing the antibiotic
- OD_blank = optical density of the blank, being the inoculated antibiotic solvent
- OD_positive control = optical density of the positive control, being inoculated medium without antibiotic

The blank is the solvent the antibiotic was dissolved in, inoculated in the
same way as the samples, for example 50% ethanol for ampicillin. Subtracting it
removes both the background of the medium and any effect of the solvent itself.
Dividing by the positive control expresses the result as a fraction of
unrestricted growth, so that plates read on different days remain comparable. A
value of 0% is growth equal to the positive control and 100% is no growth at
all.

**Worked example.** For a well at OD₆₃₀ 0.182, a blank of 0.062 and a positive
control of 0.742: (1 − (0.182 − 0.062) / (0.742 − 0.062)) × 100 =
(1 − 0.120 / 0.680) × 100 = 82.4%. This well does not reach the 90% threshold,
so it is below the IC₉₀.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-a). *Ampicillin sodium salt* (Product No. A9518) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sial/a9518
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-b). *Dimethyl sulfoxide* (Product No. D8418) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/d8418
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-c). *Ethanol absolute for analysis EMSURE* (Product No. 1.00983) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/mm/100983
- Veiga, A., Toledo, M. D. G. T., Rossa, L. S., Mengarda, M., Stofella, N. C. F., Oliveira, L. J., Gonçalves, A. G., & Murakami, F. S. (2019). Colorimetric microdilution assay: Validation of a standard method for determination of MIC, IC50%, and IC90% of antimicrobial compounds. *Journal of Microbiological Methods, 162*, 50–61. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.mimet.2019.05.003

</ExpandableText>

### Transfer into *P. vulgatus* DSM1447

<ExpandableText title="Electroporation">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/electroporation-1.pdf" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes the transformation of electrocompetent *Escherichia
coli* by electroporation. A short, high-voltage pulse makes the cell membrane
transiently permeable, so that DNA in the surrounding solution enters the cell.
For *E. coli* the pulse is delivered at a field strength of approximately
18 kV/cm with a time constant of about 5 ms (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

Electroporation gives higher transformation efficiencies than chemical
transformation for most gram-negative bacteria, but it is far more sensitive to
salt: ions carried over with the cells or with the DNA lower the resistance of
the sample and cause arcing. Everything that follows about washing, buffers and
temperature is in service of that.

The procedure takes two days: the transformation and plating on the first day,
and the verification on the second.

#### Attributions

This protocol is a rewritten version of the standard operating procedure
"BSB.006. Transform DNA into electrocompetent TOP10 *E. coli* cells", prepared
by Caroline Thyssen Petersen and reviewed by Ruben Vazquez-Uribe. The BSB
series is the set of Bacterial Synthetic Biology standard operating procedures
that Ruben Vazquez-Uribe, principal investigator of the iGEM KU Leuven 2026
team, wrote for his students in Denmark.

The electrical conditions, the washing requirements and the guidance on arcing
are taken from the electroporation manual of the instrument manufacturer
(Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

The iGEM KU Leuven 2026 team used electroporation to transform *E. coli* in the
toolkit workstream.

#### Materials

**Strains**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Electrocompetent *E. coli* | 50 µL per transformation; commercial cells or cells prepared in house |

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Purified DNA | Approximately 15 ng in 1 µL; in water or TE, not in a salt-containing buffer |
| SOC medium | 500 µL per transformation |
| LB agar plates with the appropriate antibiotic | One per transformation |
| LB broth, or the broth matching the strain | For concentrating the cells if all of them are to be plated |
| MQ water (Milli-Q), cold | For splitting an aliquot of cells over several transformations |
| Glycerol, 10% (v/v), sterile and ice-cold | For washing, when the cells are prepared in house |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Electroporation cuvettes, 1 mm gap | One per transformation; chilled on ice for at least 20 minutes |
| Microcentrifuge tubes, 1.5 mL | One per transformation; chilled |
| Micropipettes and sterile tips | 0.5–10 µL, 20–200 µL and 100–1000 µL |
| Ice bucket with an ice and water slurry | The cells are kept as close to 0 °C as possible |
| Spreading beads or a spreader | For plating |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Electroporator with a cuvette chamber | Set for bacterial electroporation |
| Thermomixer or shaking incubator | 37 °C, 900 rpm, for the recovery |
| Microcentrifuge | 5000 × g, for concentrating the cells before plating |
| Incubator | 37 °C, for the plates |
| Refrigerated centrifuge | 4000 × g at 4 °C, when preparing the cells in house |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

The electroporator delivers a high voltage. The cuvette is placed in the
chamber and the lid closed before the pulse is applied, and the chamber is not
opened while the instrument is charged. A cuvette that has arced is discarded.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kanamycin sulfate | 25389-94-0 | Danger. H360D: may damage the unborn child (Repr. 1B). | Wear gloves, lab coat and safety glasses. Weigh powder in a fume hood. Not to be handled during pregnancy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-c) |
| Ampicillin sodium salt | 69-52-3 | Danger. H317: may cause an allergic skin reaction. H334: may cause allergy or asthma symptoms if inhaled. | Avoid dust formation and inhalation. Not to be handled by persons with a penicillin allergy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-a) |
| Glycerol | 56-81-5 | Not classified as hazardous. | Spills are slippery: clean up immediately. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-b) |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

#### Procedure

**Day 1: preparing for the pulse**

1. Place the electroporation cuvettes on ice at least 20 minutes before
   starting.
2. Thaw the electrocompetent cells on ice for 5 to 7 minutes.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The cells are kept on ice at all times. The transformation efficiency of
*E. coli* falls roughly a hundredfold when the pulse is applied at 20 °C
instead of close to 0 °C (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

One tube of the frozen stock holds 50 µL of cells. When the transformation is
expected to be efficient, the aliquot can be split: add 100 µL of cold MQ water
to the tube and transfer 30 µL into cold tubes, which gives several
transformations from one aliquot.

</ProtocolNote>

3. Pipette 1 µL of purified DNA into the tube of cells, and flick the tube four
   or five times to mix very gently.

<ProtocolNote>

Approximately 15 ng of DNA is used. For a Gibson assembly, dilute the assembly
1 in 4 first (2 µL of assembly with 6 µL of MQ water) and mix gently by
pipetting.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The DNA must be in a low ionic strength solution such as water or TE. Salt
carried in with the DNA lowers the resistance of the sample and causes arcing;
residual salt from an ethanol precipitation is washed out of the pellet before
the DNA is dissolved (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.). The final salt concentration
of the sample is kept below about 5 meq for high-voltage operation.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: the pulse**

4. Transfer all the cells into the bottom of a cold electroporation cuvette and
   tap the suspension down so that no air gap remains.

<ProtocolNote>

The cells and the DNA are mixed in the tube rather than in the cuvette, because
the narrow gap of the cuvette prevents even mixing.

</ProtocolNote>

5. Place the cuvette in the chamber, close the lid and apply a single pulse at
   1.8 kV for a 1 mm cuvette.

<ProtocolNote>

For a 1 mm cuvette the settings are 1.8 kV with a capacitance of 25 µF and a
parallel resistance of 200 ohm; in a 2 mm cuvette the same field strength is
reached at 2.5 kV (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

6. Add 500 µL of SOC medium to the cuvette immediately after the pulse and mix
   gently with the pipette.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The transfer into outgrowth medium is the time-critical step of the protocol.
Delaying it by one minute lowers the number of transformants threefold, and by
ten minutes about twentyfold (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

7. Record the time constant and the voltage that the instrument reports.

<ProtocolNote>

The time constant should be close to 5 ms. A markedly shorter time constant, or
an audible arc, indicates that the sample resistance was too low, which is
almost always salt carried in with the cells or the DNA.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: recovery and plating**

8. Transfer the cells in SOC medium to a labelled microcentrifuge tube.
9. Recover the cells for 1 hour at 37 °C and 900 rpm in a thermomixer.
10. Plate approximately 70 µL of the transformed cells on an LB agar plate with
    the appropriate antibiotic.

<ProtocolNote>

The volume plated depends on the efficiency expected. When the efficiency is
expected to be low, all the cells can be plated by concentrating them:
centrifuge at 5000 × g for 5 minutes, discard the supernatant and resuspend the
pellet in 100 µL of fresh broth.

</ProtocolNote>

11. Incubate the plates overnight at 37 °C.

**Day 2: verification and storage**

12. Check the transformants by colony PCR (see the protocol "Colony PCR").
13. Store the plates under the conditions appropriate to the strain, or prepare
    cryostocks of the verified transformants (see the protocol "Cryostock
    Generation").

**Preparing electrocompetent cells in house**

14. Inoculate 5 mL of a fresh overnight culture into 500 mL of broth and grow
    at 37 °C with vigorous shaking to an OD₆₀₀ of approximately 0.5 to 0.7
    (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

<ProtocolNote>

The highest efficiencies are obtained from cells harvested in early to mid log
phase. As an *E. coli* culture reaches stationary phase the efficiency falls
steeply (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

15. Chill the culture on ice for approximately 20 minutes and keep the cells as
    close to 0 °C as possible from this point on, chilling every container
    before use.
16. Centrifuge at 4000 × g for 15 minutes at 4 °C and pour off the supernatant
    completely.

<ProtocolNote>

It is better to lose a few cells with the supernatant than to leave any
supernatant behind, because the growth medium is what carries the ions (Bio-Rad
Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

17. Wash the pellet by resuspending it in ice-cold 10% glycerol and
    centrifuging again, at least three times, reducing the volume at each step.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The washes are what make the cells electrocompetent. Cells that still carry
growth medium arc at high field strength. Washing is done with water or a
non-ionic solution; 10% glycerol is convenient because it is also the
cryoprotectant for storage (Bio-Rad Laboratories, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

18. Resuspend the final pellet in a small volume of ice-cold 10% glycerol,
    aliquot, freeze on dry ice and store at −70 °C or below.

<ProtocolNote>

Cells stored this way are stable for at least six months (Bio-Rad Laboratories,
n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The standard operating procedure this protocol is based on is written for
commercial electrocompetent TOP10 cells, which are stored in the −80 °C
freezer. The in-house preparation section was added from the manufacturer
manual, for when commercial cells are not available.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

Which strains the team electroporated, and with which constructs, still needs
to be recorded here.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The header of the standard operating procedure carries the number BSB.004 while
the document is BSB.006; the header template appears not to have been updated.
The same mismatch appears in the colony PCR standard operating procedure.

</ProtocolNote>

<Callout title="Still to be added">

The catalogue number of the competent cells and of the cuvettes, and the model
of the electroporator, still need to be added.

</Callout>

- Bio-Rad Laboratories. (n.d.). *Gene Pulser Xcell electroporation system instruction manual: Section 5, Factors affecting electroporation, and Section 6, Electroporation of bacterial cells*. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.bio-rad.com/
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-a). *Ampicillin sodium salt* (Product No. A9518) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sial/a9518
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-b). *Glycerol* (Product No. G5516) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/g5516
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.-c). *Kanamycin sulfate from Streptomyces kanamyceticus* (Product No. K1377) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/k1377
