### Reporter Characterization

<ExpandableText title="Rhamnose Induction and Medium Optimization">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/rhamnose-induction-and-medium-optimization-3.pdf" workbookTab="Rhamnose induction" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes the preparation of L-rhamnose stock solutions and of
induced cultures of *Phocaeicola vulgatus* carrying rhamnose-inducible pNBU2
constructs, together with the medium variants that were compared during
troubleshooting.

Rhamnose is heat sensitive, so it is filter-sterilized and added to the medium
after autoclaving. The medium variants compare mGAM with the minimal medium for
*P. vulgatus*, and glucose with rhamnose as the carbon source, at matched and
at raised molar concentrations.

The minimal medium used here is the anaerobic medium for *P. vulgatus*, which
contains hemin. It is not the aerobic minimal medium for *E. coli* used in the
liquid bile salt assay.

#### Materials

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| L-rhamnose monohydrate | 182.2 mg per mL of 1 M stock; the anhydrous form requires 164.2 mg |
| Water, sterile | 1 mL per mL of stock |
| mGAM broth | As required |
| Minimal medium for *P. vulgatus* | As required (see the protocol "Preparation of Minimal Medium for *P. vulgatus*"); the anaerobic medium with hemin, not the *E. coli* medium of the liquid bile salt assay |
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 carrying the pNBU2 construct, and the plasmid-free control strain | One colony per culture |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Syringe filters, 0.22 µm, with syringes | For sterilization of the rhamnose stock |
| Sterile tubes | For the stock and the cultures |
| Micropipettes and sterile tips | As required |
| Weighing boats and spatula | As required |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Precision balance | Readability 0.1 mg |
| Autoclave | For the medium |
| pH meter | For the minimal medium |
| Anaerobic chamber and incubator | 37 °C |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L-Rhamnose monohydrate | 10030-85-0 | The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Avoid dust formation when weighing. | Supplier SDS |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

<Callout title="Still to be added">

The supplier and catalogue number of the L-rhamnose, and the CAS number of the
form in use, still need to be added. The CAS number given in the safety table
is that of the monohydrate and should be checked against the bottle.

</Callout>

#### Procedure

**Preparing the rhamnose stock**

1. Weigh the L-rhamnose required for a 1 M stock solution (Table 2): 182.2 mg
   of the monohydrate, or 164.2 mg of the anhydrous form, per mL of water.

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Mass of L-rhamnose for a stock solution."
  workbookTab="Rhamnose induction"
  rows={[
    { key: "conc", label: "Stock concentration", value: 1, unit: "M", input: true },
    { key: "volume", label: "Stock volume", value: 1, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "molarMass", label: "Molar mass (monohydrate 182.17; anhydrous 164.16)", value: 182.17, unit: "g/mol", input: true },
    { key: "mass", label: "L-rhamnose to weigh", unit: "mg", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.conc * (v.volume / 1000) * v.molarMass * 1000 },
  ]}
/>

2. Dissolve the rhamnose in water.
3. Pass the stock through a 0.22 µm syringe filter into a sterile tube.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The stock is filter-sterilized and not autoclaved, because autoclaving degrades
the sugar. Filtration also keeps the stock sterile.

</ProtocolNote>

**Preparing the induced cultures**

4. Prepare the medium according to the manufacturer's instructions, without
   rhamnose, and autoclave it as normal (typically 121 °C for 15 minutes).
5. Let the medium cool to 50–60 °C before anything heat-sensitive is added.
6. Calculate the volume of rhamnose stock required for the intended final
   concentration and culture volume (Table 3), and add it to the cooled medium
   so that the culture reaches the intended final volume.

<ProtocolNote>

For 15 mM in 10 mL from a 1 M stock, 150 µL of stock is added to 9.85 mL of
medium. For 10 mM in 10 mL, 100 µL is added to 9.9 mL.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="Volume of rhamnose stock per culture (C1 × V1 = C2 × V2)."
  workbookTab="Rhamnose induction"
  rows={[
    { key: "c1", label: "Stock concentration (C1)", value: 1000, unit: "mM", input: true },
    { key: "c2", label: "Required final concentration (C2)", value: 15, unit: "mM", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Culture volume (V2)", value: 10, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "v1", label: "Stock to add (V1)", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => ((v.c2 * v.v2) / v.c1) * 1000 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.v2 * 1000 - v.v1 },
  ]}
/>

7. Mix well, inoculate with a single colony and incubate overnight at 37 °C
   under anaerobic conditions.

**Medium variants compared during troubleshooting**

8. To compare rhamnose with glucose at the same molar concentration, convert
   the glucose content of the minimal medium into a molar concentration and
   weigh the equivalent amount of rhamnose (Table 4).

<ProtocolNote>

The minimal medium for *P. vulgatus* contains 0.2 g/L glucose, which
corresponds to 1.11 mM.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={4}
  title="Matching the rhamnose concentration to the glucose concentration."
  workbookTab="Rhamnose induction"
  rows={[
    { key: "gGlucose", label: "Glucose concentration in the medium", value: 0.2, unit: "g/L", input: true },
    { key: "mGlucose", label: "Molar mass of glucose", value: 180.16, unit: "g/mol", input: true },
    { key: "cEquiv", label: "Equivalent molar concentration", unit: "mM", decimals: 2, compute: (v) => (v.gGlucose / v.mGlucose) * 1000 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium volume", value: 30, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "mRha", label: "Molar mass of L-rhamnose monohydrate", value: 182.17, unit: "g/mol", input: true },
    { key: "mass", label: "L-rhamnose to weigh for that concentration", unit: "mg", decimals: 2, compute: (v) => (v.cEquiv / 1000) * (v.vMedium / 1000) * v.mRha * 1000 },
  ]}
/>

9. Prepare the culture set required for the comparison, including the
   plasmid-free and uninduced controls.

**Table 5.** Medium variants compared in the troubleshooting of 7 September 2026.

| No. | Medium | Rhamnose | Strain |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | mGAM, 10 mL | 15 mM | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 2 | Minimal medium, 10 mL | 10 mM (glucose retained) | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 3 | Minimal medium, 10 mL | 1.11 mM, replacing glucose | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 4 | Minimal medium, 10 mL | 10 mM, replacing glucose | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 5 | mGAM, 1 mL | None | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 6 | mGAM, 1 mL | None | DSM1447 |
| 7 | Minimal medium, 1 mL | None | DSM1447 with pNBU2 |
| 8 | Minimal medium, 1 mL | None | DSM1447 |

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

The first induction, on 19 August 2026, used 10 mM rhamnose in 1 mL of mGAM,
which corresponds to 1.82 mg of the monohydrate.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="September 2026">

The medium comparison of 7 September 2026 raised the mGAM condition to 15 mM
and added minimal medium conditions in which rhamnose either supplemented
glucose or replaced it, at the glucose-equivalent concentration (1.11 mM) and
at 10 mM.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="September 2026">

The minimal medium with glucose and rhamnose was brought to the correct pH on
9 September 2026 but was not de-oxygenized overnight. It was used nonetheless,
which should be taken into account when the results of that run are
interpreted.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The draft gives 6.23 mg of rhamnose for the glucose-equivalent condition
(1.11 mM in 30 mL). Recalculation gives 6.07 mg; the difference comes from
rounding in the draft. The value in Table 4 is the recalculated one.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Mass of L-rhamnose for a stock solution**

*m* = *c* × *V* × *M*

in which:

- *m* = mass to weigh (g)
- *c* = stock concentration (mol/L)
- *V* = stock volume (L)
- *M* = molar mass (g/mol)

L-rhamnose monohydrate has a molar mass of 182.17 g/mol and the anhydrous form
164.16 g/mol, so the form on the bottle must be checked.

**Worked example.** For 1 mL of a 1 M stock from the monohydrate: *m* =
1 × 0.001 × 182.17 = 0.18217 g = 182.2 mg. The anhydrous form requires
164.2 mg.

**Volume of rhamnose stock per culture**

C1 × V1 = C2 × V2 → V1 = (C2 × V2) / C1

in which:

- C1 = concentration of the rhamnose stock
- V1 = volume of stock to add
- C2 = required final concentration
- V2 = final culture volume

The volume of medium is V2 − V1, so that the culture reaches exactly the
intended final volume.

**Worked example.** For 15 mM in 10 mL from a 1 M (1000 mM) stock: V1 =
(15 × 10) / 1000 = 0.15 mL = 150 µL of stock, added to 9850 µL of medium.

**Matching the rhamnose concentration to the glucose concentration**

*c* = (*g* / *M*) × 1000  and  *m* = *c* × *V* × *M*r

in which:

- *g* = glucose concentration in the medium (g/L)
- *M* = molar mass of glucose (180.16 g/mol)
- *c* = equivalent molar concentration (mM)
- *V* = volume of medium (L)
- *M*r = molar mass of L-rhamnose monohydrate (182.17 g/mol)
- *m* = mass of L-rhamnose to weigh

This converts the glucose content of the minimal medium into a molar
concentration, so that rhamnose can replace glucose on a mole-for-mole basis.

**Worked example.** Glucose at 0.2 g/L corresponds to (0.2 / 180.16) × 1000 =
1.11 mM. For 30 mL of medium: *m* = 0.00111 × 0.030 × 182.17 = 0.00607 g =
6.07 mg of L-rhamnose monohydrate.

</ProtocolCalculations>

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Plate Reader Sample Preparation">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/plate-reader-sample-preparation-2.pdf" workbookTab="Plate reader prep" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes how cultures are prepared and normalized before an
endpoint measurement in the plate reader. Every culture is brought to the same
starting optical density, so that the measured signal reflects the construct
and not the amount of biomass in the well.

It is used for the promoter-reporter screen and can be used for any
plate-reader measurement that compares strains against each other.

#### Materials

**Strains**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 carrying the constructs, and the control strains | At least three colonies per construct |

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| mGAM broth with the appropriate antibiotic | 2–5 mL per culture |
| mGAM agar plates with the appropriate antibiotic | One per strain |
| Sterile water | For the border wells of the plate |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Culture tubes | One per biological replicate |
| Microtiter plate matching the measurement | Black with a transparent flat bottom for fluorescence and OD₆₀₀ |
| Cuvettes or a clear plate | For the optical density of the overnight cultures |
| Micropipettes, a multichannel pipette and sterile tips | As required |
| Sticky plate seals, transparent | One per plate; seals rather than lids |
| Reservoir for the multichannel pipette | As required |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Anaerobic chamber with incubator | 37 °C |
| Microplate reader | OD₆₀₀ and the measurement channels in use |
| Insulated container | For transfer between chambers |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kanamycin sulfate | 25389-94-0 | Danger. H360D: may damage the unborn child (Repr. 1B). | Wear gloves, lab coat and safety glasses. Weigh powder in a fume hood. Not to be handled during pregnancy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.) |
| Erythromycin | 114-07-8 | The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Avoid dust formation. Stock solutions are prepared in ethanol in a fume hood. | Supplier SDS |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

#### Procedure

**Day 1: plates**

1. Streak each strain carrying a construct, and the control strains, on
   selective agar plates and grow them overnight at 37 °C in the anaerobic
   chamber.

<ProtocolNote>

Growing from a plate rather than directly from a cryostock gives single
colonies, so that separate colonies can be used as biological replicates.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 2: overnight cultures**

2. Inoculate one culture per biological replicate from a separate colony, in
   medium with the appropriate antibiotic.

<ProtocolNote>

At least three colonies per construct are used, so that the variation between
replicates is the variation between colonies and not between pipetting steps.

</ProtocolNote>

3. Grow the cultures overnight at 37 °C in the anaerobic chamber.

**Day 3: normalization and plating**

4. Resuspend each overnight culture thoroughly and measure its OD₆₀₀, using
   uninoculated medium as the blank.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

*P. vulgatus* sediments quickly, so the culture is resuspended immediately
before the reading. A reading taken from a settled culture underestimates the
density and the dilution that follows will be wrong.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

A reading above approximately 0.8 lies outside the linear range of most
readers; a dense culture is diluted in medium and the reading multiplied by the
dilution factor.

</ProtocolNote>

5. Dilute each culture to an OD₆₀₀ of 0.3 in a fresh plate, calculating the
   volume of culture and of medium per well (Table 2).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Dilution of the culture to the target OD₆₀₀."
  workbookTab="Plate reader prep"
  rows={[
    { key: "od1", label: "Measured OD₆₀₀ of the overnight culture", value: 1.2, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "od2", label: "Target OD₆₀₀ in the measurement plate", value: 0.3, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "vWell", label: "Final volume per well", value: 200, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "vCulture", label: "Culture to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.od2 * v.vWell) / v.od1 },
    { key: "vMedium", label: "Medium to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.vWell - v.vCulture },
    { key: "nWells", label: "Number of wells per culture", value: 3, unit: "wells", input: true },
    { key: "vPerConstruct", label: "Culture required per construct", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.vCulture * v.nWells },
  ]}
/>

When a whole plate is seeded at once, the volumes for every well are calculated
in the 96-well seeding grid: `Protocol_Calculators.xlsx`, tab "Plate seeding".
The measured OD₆₀₀ of each well is entered in the first grid, and the volume of
culture and of medium for that well is read from the second and third grids.

<ProtocolNote>

The grid subtracts the medium blank from every reading. It marks a well "LOW"
when the culture is already at or below the target optical density and cannot
be diluted to it, and "n/a" when the blank-corrected reading is zero or
negative.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

An OD₆₀₀ of 0.3 was the starting density used by the team. Every construct is
set to the same value, which is what makes the readings comparable.

</ProtocolNote>

6. Fill the outer wells of the plate with sterile water.

<ProtocolNote>

The water border limits evaporation from the sample wells and leaves 60 usable
wells on a 96-well plate.

</ProtocolNote>

7. Include the control wells on the same plate: the strain without construct
   for the autofluorescence baseline, and medium without cells for the
   background.
8. Seal the plate with a transparent sticky seal rather than a lid.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

A lid allows evaporation over the course of an incubation, which changes the
optical density and the signal in the outer wells first.

</ProtocolNote>

**Measurement**

9. Transfer the plate to the reader in a closed insulated container if the
   reader is in another chamber.
10. Shake or mix the plate immediately before every optical density reading.
11. Read the plate following the protocol of the measurement in use (see the
    protocol "Promoter-Reporter Screen").

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

This procedure was originally written inside the conjugation protocol, where
the optical density of the mating cultures is normalized in the same way. It
has been separated so that it can be cited from any plate-reader experiment.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The medium blank of the run of 2 October 2026 read an OD₆₀₀ of 0.541. mGAM is a
dark medium, so the blank is high and subtracting it matters: a reading of 1.5
corresponds to a corrected density of about 0.96, which is roughly a third
lower.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

In that run several wells read close to the blank value, which the seeding grid
marks as "LOW" or "n/a". Those cultures had not grown enough to be diluted to
the target density.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Dilution of the culture to the target optical density**

OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2 → V1 = (OD2 × V2) / OD1

in which:

- OD1 = measured OD₆₀₀ of the overnight culture
- V1 = volume of culture to add (µL)
- OD2 = target OD₆₀₀ in the measurement plate
- V2 = final volume per well (µL)

The volume of medium per well is V2 − V1. Every culture is brought to the same
starting optical density, so that the measured signal reflects the construct
and not the amount of biomass.

**Worked example.** To reach OD₆₀₀ 0.3 in 200 µL from a culture at OD₆₀₀ 1.2:
V1 = (0.3 × 200) / 1.2 = 50.0 µL of culture and 150.0 µL of medium. For three
wells per construct, 150.0 µL of culture is needed.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.). *Kanamycin sulfate from Streptomyces kanamyceticus* (Product No. K1377) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 6, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/k1377

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Promoter-Reporter Screen">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/promoter-reporter-screen-2.pdf" workbookTab="Promoter screen" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes the plate-reader screen used to compare the strength of
the promoters of the promoter library in *Phocaeicola vulgatus* DSM1447. Each
construct couples a promoter to one of four reporters (sfGFP, mCherry, KOFP-7
or NanoLuc), and the signal of each well is normalized to the optical density
of that well.

The screen is an endpoint measurement. A kinetic assay is not possible with the
equipment available, because the team has no anaerobic plate reader for
measurements over time.

The oxygen requirement of the reporter determines the handling. sfGFP and
mCherry form their chromophore in an autocatalytic reaction that requires
molecular oxygen, so cells grown anaerobically need an aerobic maturation step
before reading. KOFP-7 is a flavin-binding fluorescent protein derived from
*Pseudomonas putida* and fluoresces without oxygen (FPbase, n.d.-a), so it can
be read directly. NanoLuc needs oxygen only at the moment of the light-emitting
reaction, which is the moment the substrate is added.

#### Materials

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 strains carrying the promoter-reporter constructs | Erythromycin resistance; at least 3 colonies per construct |
| *E. coli* DH5α with the constitutive promoter BBa_J23100 and sfGFP | Kanamycin resistance; positive control |
| *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 without construct | Autofluorescence control |
| mGAM broth and LB broth | Also used without cells as medium-only blanks |
| Erythromycin and kanamycin stock solutions | For the selective media |
| Nano-Glo Luciferase Assay Substrate and Buffer | Promega, cat. no. N1110; for the NanoLuc constructs |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Black 96-well plate with flat transparent bottom | For the fluorescence and OD₆₀₀ measurements |
| White opaque 96-well plate | For the luminescence measurements |
| Clear 96-well plate | For the parallel OD₆₀₀ measurement of the luminescence samples |
| Culture tubes | One per biological replicate |
| Micropipettes, multichannel pipette and sterile tips | As required |
| Sterile water | For the border wells of the plate |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Microplate reader | CLARIOstar Plus; OD₆₀₀, fluorescence and luminescence |
| Anaerobic chamber and incubator | 37 °C |

**Software**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| CLARIOstar reader control software | For the measurement protocols, the plate layout, the focus adjustment and the gain adjustment |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kanamycin sulfate | 25389-94-0 | Danger. H360D: may damage the unborn child (Repr. 1B). | Wear gloves, lab coat and safety glasses. Weigh powder in a fume hood. Not to be handled during pregnancy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.) |
| Erythromycin | 114-07-8 | The SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Avoid dust formation. Stock solutions are prepared in ethanol in a fume hood. | Supplier SDS |
| Nano-Glo Luciferase Assay Reagent | Mixture | Contains a lysis buffer; the SDS of the product in use should be consulted. | Wear gloves and safety glasses. | Promega (n.d.) Technical manual; SDS on the product page |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

<Callout title="Still to be added">

The supplier and catalogue numbers of the plates, the antibiotics and the
Nano-Glo kit still need to be added.

</Callout>

#### Procedure

**Planning the plate**

1. Determine the number of wells and plates required for the constructs, the
   controls and the replicates (Table 2).

<ProtocolNote>

At least three biological replicates (three separate colonies) per construct
are used, each with two or three technical replicates where the plate allows
it.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Plate layout: number of wells required."
  workbookTab="Promoter screen"
  rows={[
    { key: "n", label: "Number of constructs and controls", value: 21, unit: "samples", input: true },
    { key: "bio", label: "Biological replicates per construct", value: 3, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "tech", label: "Technical replicates per biological replicate", value: 1, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "wells", label: "Sample wells required", unit: "wells", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.n * v.bio * v.tech },
    { key: "perPlate", label: "Wells available per plate (96-well plate with a water border)", value: 60, unit: "wells", input: true },
    { key: "plates", label: "Plates required", unit: "plates", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => Math.ceil(v.wells / v.perPlate) },
  ]}
/>

2. Include the controls on every plate: *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 without construct
   for the autofluorescence baseline, medium without cells for the background,
   and the constitutive *E. coli* construct as a positive control.
3. Fill the outer wells of the plate with sterile water.

<ProtocolNote>

The water border limits evaporation from the sample wells during incubation and
leaves 60 usable wells on a 96-well plate.

</ProtocolNote>

**Preparing the samples**

4. Streak the strains on selective agar plates and grow them anaerobically.
5. Inoculate two colonies per construct into liquid broth and incubate
   overnight.
6. Make a 1:100 subculture the next day, transfer it into the 96-well plate and
   incubate overnight.

<ProtocolNote>

A black flat-bottomed plate with a transparent bottom is used, so that
fluorescence and OD₆₀₀ can be read in the same wells.

</ProtocolNote>

**Measuring sfGFP, mCherry and KOFP-7**

7. Take the plate out of the anaerobic chamber.
8. For sfGFP and mCherry, expose the cells to air for a fixed time before
   reading, and keep that time identical for every promoter variant and
   replicate.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

sfGFP and mCherry require oxygen for chromophore maturation, so cells that
never see oxygen read as "off" regardless of the strength of the promoter. The
maturation time must be the same across all variants and replicates, otherwise
the comparison between promoters is confounded by maturation. An exposure of 30
to 90 minutes was used by the team.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote>

KOFP-7 needs no maturation step and can be read directly, including from an
anaerobic culture (FPbase, n.d.-a).

</ProtocolNote>

9. Read OD₆₀₀ and fluorescence in the same wells, using the excitation and
   emission settings of Table 3.

**Table 3.** Excitation and emission maxima of the reporters, from FPbase
(FPbase, n.d.-c; FPbase, n.d.-b; FPbase, n.d.-a).

| Reporter | Excitation | Emission | Oxygen requirement |
|---|---|---|---|
| sfGFP | 485 nm | 510 nm | Required for chromophore maturation; maturation time approximately 14 min |
| mCherry | 587 nm | 610 nm | Required for chromophore maturation; maturation time approximately 15 min |
| KOFP-7 | 450 nm | 496 nm | Not required; flavin-binding, fluoresces anaerobically |
| NanoLuc | Luminescence | Luminescence | Required only for the light-emitting reaction, at substrate addition |

<ProtocolNote>

The excitation and emission maxima above are the values listed on FPbase for
each protein. The filter or monochromator settings of the reader should be set
from the full spectra on FPbase rather than from the maxima alone.

</ProtocolNote>

**Measuring NanoLuc**

10. Grow the strains anaerobically to the intended optical density and read
    OD₆₀₀ in a clear plate, or use a parallel OD plate.

<ProtocolNote>

OD₆₀₀ and luminescence should not be read in the same white plate, because the
opaque wells give poor OD accuracy.

</ProtocolNote>

11. Transfer the culture into a white luminescence plate.
12. Prepare the Nano-Glo Luciferase Assay Reagent by combining one volume of
    substrate with 50 volumes of buffer, and let the reagent and the samples
    equilibrate to room temperature (Promega, n.d.).
13. Add a volume of reagent equal to the volume of culture in each well
    (Promega, n.d.).
14. Mix gently and wait at least 3 minutes before measuring the luminescence
    (Promega, n.d.).

<ProtocolNote>

The signal is a glow-type signal that decays slowly, with a half-life of
approximately 120 minutes at room temperature (Promega, n.d.). The whole plate
should nevertheless be read in one pass, so that every well is read at a
comparable time after the addition of the reagent.

</ProtocolNote>

15. Blank-subtract with medium and substrate, and background-subtract with the
    promoterless construct and substrate.

**Setting up the plate reader**

16. Open the reader software and log in under the user account.
17. Enter the brand and format of the plate in use into the measurement
    protocol.
18. Create a new protocol and add it as a quick access button.
19. Add the plate layout, assigning the correct measurement to the
    corresponding wells.
20. Select a central well for the focus adjustment, and leave the focus
    adjustment switched on so that it runs before every protocol.
21. Perform a gain adjustment on a well expected to give a high signal, which
    acts as the ceiling.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The gain adjustment is performed only the first time the protocol is run. Once
the gain is set and the first protocol is complete, the gain must not be
changed, because readings taken at different gains cannot be compared. The
usable range of the gain is roughly 70 to 90; readings below 1000 or above
260,000 are not reliable and indicate that the gain needs to be readjusted.

</ProtocolNote>

22. Set the excitation and emission ranges for each reporter (Table 3).
23. Run the measurement protocols in order: OD₆₀₀ of the recipient strains,
    OD₆₀₀ of the *E. coli* control, then the fluorescence channels.

**Processing the data**

24. Subtract the medium-only blank from the fluorescence and the optical
    density of each well, and subtract the autofluorescence control to remove
    the intrinsic fluorescence of the cells.
25. Normalize the corrected fluorescence of each well to the corrected optical
    density of that same well (Table 4).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={4}
  title="Normalized fluorescence."
  workbookTab="Promoter screen"
  rows={[
    { key: "fSample", label: "Fluorescence of the sample well", value: 45000, unit: "RFU", input: true },
    { key: "fBlank", label: "Fluorescence of the medium-only blank", value: 1200, unit: "RFU", input: true },
    { key: "odSample", label: "OD₆₀₀ of the sample well", value: 0.45, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "odBlank", label: "OD₆₀₀ of the medium-only blank", value: 0.05, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "fAuto", label: "Fluorescence of the cell-only autofluorescence control", value: 3000, unit: "RFU", input: true },
    { key: "odCorrected", label: "Corrected OD₆₀₀", unit: "–", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => v.odSample - v.odBlank },
    { key: "fBlankCorrected", label: "Blank-corrected fluorescence", unit: "RFU", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.fSample - v.fBlank },
    { key: "normBlank", label: "Normalized fluorescence (blank corrected)", unit: "RFU/OD", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.fBlankCorrected / v.odCorrected },
    { key: "fAutoCorrected", label: "Autofluorescence-corrected fluorescence", unit: "RFU", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.fSample - v.fAuto },
    { key: "normAuto", label: "Normalized fluorescence (autofluorescence corrected)", unit: "RFU/OD", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.fAutoCorrected / v.odCorrected },
  ]}
/>

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="September 2026">

The screen of 18 September 2026 was set up as an oxygen-maturation test,
comparing the maturation of sfGFP and mCherry at different times of oxygen
exposure, with six constructs, the *E. coli* positive control, the
plasmid-free control and a medium-only control.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

In the run of 2 October 2026, 18 constructs were measured with four replicates
in a 96-well plate with a water border. Sample 17 was not available, so sample
19 was placed in its position in column 8; wells 19.1 and 19.2 were seeded from
a tube labelled as the DSM1447 control. The plate map is therefore not a direct
read of the sample numbers.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

Not every construct was on the plate of 29 September 2026; the GFP constructs
of BT, PBT, Cfx and PAM and the PAM NanoLuc construct were missing from that
run.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The draft noted that measurements greater than 1000 and less than 260,000 are
not to be trusted, which is the reverse of the intended meaning. Readings
outside that range are the unreliable ones.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The NanoLuc measurements are to be performed in the collaborating laboratory,
because the equipment is not available in the team laboratory.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Number of wells and plates required**

*W* = *n* × *b* × *t*  and  *P* = *W* / *c* (rounded up)

in which:

- *W* = sample wells required
- *n* = number of constructs and controls
- *b* = biological replicates per construct
- *t* = technical replicates per biological replicate
- *c* = usable wells per plate
- *P* = number of plates

When the outer wells of a 96-well plate are filled with water to limit
evaporation, 60 of the 96 wells remain usable.

**Worked example.** For 21 samples in 3 biological replicates without technical
replicates: *W* = 21 × 3 × 1 = 63 wells, which needs 63 / 60 = 2 plates.

**Normalized fluorescence**

*F*norm = (*F*sample − *F*control) / (OD sample − OD blank)

in which:

- *F*sample = fluorescence of the sample well (RFU)
- *F*control = fluorescence of the control well (RFU): the medium-only blank, or the cell-only autofluorescence control
- OD sample = OD₆₀₀ of the sample well
- OD blank = OD₆₀₀ of the medium-only blank

Normalization to the optical density expresses the signal per unit of biomass,
so that constructs with different growth can be compared. Two corrections are
possible: subtracting the medium-only blank removes the background of the
medium, and subtracting the autofluorescence control removes the intrinsic
fluorescence of the cells as well. The same correction must be applied to every
construct in a comparison.

**Worked example.** For a well with 45,000 RFU at OD₆₀₀ 0.45, a medium blank of
1200 RFU at OD₆₀₀ 0.05 and an autofluorescence control of 3000 RFU: the
corrected OD is 0.45 − 0.05 = 0.400. Blank-corrected: (45,000 − 1200) / 0.400 =
109,500 RFU/OD. Autofluorescence-corrected: (45,000 − 3000) / 0.400 =
105,000 RFU/OD.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- FPbase. (n.d.-a). *KOFP-7*. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.fpbase.org/protein/kofp-7/
- FPbase. (n.d.-b). *mCherry*. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.fpbase.org/protein/mcherry/
- FPbase. (n.d.-c). *Superfolder GFP*. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.fpbase.org/protein/superfolder-gfp/
- Promega. (n.d.). *Nano-Glo luciferase assay system* (Technical Manual TM369). Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.promega.com/-/media/files/resources/protocols/technical-manuals/101/nanoglo-luciferase-assay-system-protocol.pdf
- Sigma-Aldrich. (n.d.). *Kanamycin sulfate from Streptomyces kanamyceticus* (Product No. K1377) [Safety data sheet]. Retrieved October 11, 2026, from https://www.sigmaaldrich.com/BE/en/sds/sigma/k1377

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Fluorescence Microscopy">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/fluorescence-microscopy-2.pdf" workbookTab="Microscopy" />

#### Introduction

This protocol describes the preparation of *Phocaeicola vulgatus* cells for
fluorescence microscopy on agarose pads, used to check the expression of
fluorescent reporters from the pNBU2 constructs at the single-cell level.

Imaging is performed on an inverted microscope with oil immersion, using phase
contrast for morphological clarity alongside the fluorescence channels.

#### Materials

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Overnight cultures of the strains to be imaged | 1 mL per sample |
| Phosphate-buffered saline (PBS), 1×, sterile | Approximately 4 mL per sample (three washes and the final resuspension) |
| Agarose | 0.075 g for 5 mL of a 1.5% solution |
| Vaseline, lanolin and paraffin | Mixed in equal parts to seal the coverslip |
| Immersion oil | For the objective |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Microscope slides and coverslips | One each per sample |
| Microcentrifuge tubes, 1.5 mL | One per sample |
| Micropipettes and tips | 20–200 µL and 100–1000 µL |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Inverted fluorescence microscope with phase contrast and oil immersion objective | With the filter sets matching the reporters in use |
| Microcentrifuge | 5000 × g |
| Heating block or microwave | For dissolving the agarose |
| Precision balance | Readability 0.001 g |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

Molten agarose can cause burns. The sealing mixture is heated before use and is
handled with care.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |
| Agarose | 9012-36-6 | Not classified as hazardous. | Molten agarose can superheat and boil over: wear thermal gloves and safety glasses. | Thermo Fisher Scientific (n.d.) Product page; SDS under "Documents" |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

#### Procedure

**Preparing the cells**

1. Determine the volume of culture required to obtain the intended optical
   density after resuspension (Table 2).

<ProtocolNote>

As a guide, 1 mL of culture at an OD₆₀₀ of 0.5 is resuspended in 1 mL of 1×
PBS.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Resuspension of the cells to a defined OD₆₀₀."
  workbookTab="Microscopy"
  rows={[
    { key: "od1", label: "Measured OD₆₀₀ of the culture", value: 1, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "od2", label: "Target OD₆₀₀ after resuspension", value: 0.5, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "vFinal", label: "Final volume in PBS", value: 1000, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "vCulture", label: "Culture to pellet", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => (v.od2 * v.vFinal) / v.od1 },
    { key: "washes", label: "Number of PBS washes", value: 3, unit: "washes", input: true },
    { key: "vPBS", label: "PBS required per sample", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.vFinal * (v.washes + 1) },
  ]}
/>

2. Pellet the cells by centrifugation at 5000 × g for 5 minutes.
3. Discard the supernatant and resuspend the pellet in 1 mL of 1× PBS.
4. Repeat steps 2 and 3 twice more, for three washes in total.

**Preparing the agarose pads**

5. Prepare a 1.5% (w/v) agarose solution in 1× PBS (Table 3) and dissolve it
   completely.

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="Agarose pad."
  workbookTab="Microscopy"
  rows={[
    { key: "percent", label: "Agarose percentage (w/v)", value: 1.5, unit: "%", input: true },
    { key: "vSolution", label: "Volume of agarose solution to prepare", value: 5, unit: "mL", input: true },
    { key: "mass", label: "Agarose to weigh", unit: "g", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => (v.percent * v.vSolution) / 100 },
    { key: "vPerSlide", label: "Volume applied per slide", value: 350, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "nSlides", label: "Number of slides from this volume", unit: "slides", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => Math.floor((v.vSolution * 1000) / v.vPerSlide) },
  ]}
/>

6. Apply 300 to 400 µL of the agarose solution to a microscope slide and let it
   dry, so that the cells adhere.

<ProtocolNote>

The agarose layer is also what makes autofocus work: the autofocus and
perfect-focus systems detect a change in refraction, and the agarose produces
the shift that marks where the sample begins.

</ProtocolNote>

7. Drop the washed cell suspension onto the agarose pad.
8. Seal the slide and coverslip with a mixture of vaseline, lanolin and
   paraffin.

**Imaging**

9. Image the cells on the inverted microscope with oil immersion, using phase
   contrast for morphological clarity alongside the fluorescence channels.

<ProtocolNote>

Slides and plates require different illumination settings; slides need more
focused light.

</ProtocolNote>

10. Save the images in the project folder for the experiment, with the date and
    the strain in the file name.

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

In the session of 20 August 2026, overnight cultures were prepared the day
before for six colonies per combination: *P. vulgatus* DSM1447 with pNBU2,
DSM1447 without plasmid, and DSM1447 with pNBU2 induced with 10 mM rhamnose,
each in 1 mL of mGAM.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="August 2026">

No fluorescence could be observed in that session and some cells had died. KOFP
appears cyan under the microscope.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The images of that session are stored in the team SharePoint folder for
fluorescence microscopy. They can be linked here if they are published.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Resuspension of the cells to a defined optical density**

V1 = (OD2 × V2) / OD1  and  *V*pbs = V2 × (*w* + 1)

in which:

- V1 = volume of culture to pellet (µL)
- OD1 = measured OD₆₀₀ of the culture
- OD2 = required OD₆₀₀ after resuspension
- V2 = final volume in PBS (µL)
- *w* = number of washes
- *V*pbs = total PBS required per sample (µL)

Each wash uses one final volume of PBS, and the final resuspension uses one
more, hence the factor (*w* + 1).

**Worked example.** To obtain 1000 µL at an OD₆₀₀ of 0.5 from a culture at
OD₆₀₀ 1.0: V1 = (0.5 × 1000) / 1.0 = 500 µL of culture. With three washes,
1000 × 4 = 4000 µL of PBS is required per sample.

**Agarose for the pads**

*m* = (*p* × *V*) / 100  and  *n* = *V* × 1000 / *V*s

in which:

- *m* = mass of agarose to weigh (g)
- *p* = agarose percentage (% w/v)
- *V* = volume of agarose solution (mL)
- *V*s = volume applied per slide (µL)
- *n* = number of slides

**Worked example.** For 5 mL of 1.5% agarose: *m* = (1.5 × 5) / 100 = 0.075 g.
At 350 µL per slide this gives 5000 / 350 = 14 slides.

</ProtocolCalculations>

#### References

- Thermo Fisher Scientific. (n.d.). *UltraPure low melting point agarose* (Catalog No. 16520050) [Product information]. Retrieved October 6, 2026, from https://www.thermofisher.com/order/catalog/product/16520050

</ExpandableText>

<ExpandableText title="Fitness Cost-Based Ranking of BSH Constructs">

<ProtocolDownloads pdf="https://static.igem.wiki/teams/6299/wiki/wet-lab/protocols/fitness-cost-based-ranking-of-bsh-constructs.pdf" workbookTab="Fitness ranking" />

#### Introduction

Carrying a plasmid is rarely free. Replicating the plasmid, transcribing its
genes and folding the protein they encode all draw on the same pool of
precursors, ribosomes and chaperones that the host needs for its own growth,
and the result is a measurable reduction in growth rate relative to a strain
without the construct. This reduction is the fitness cost of the construct, and
it is the main reason an engineered population loses its construct over time in
the absence of selection (San Millan & MacLean, 2017).

The purpose of this assay is comparative rather than absolute. Each bile salt
hydrolase (BSH) construct is grown under identical conditions and plated as a
ten-fold dilution series, and the constructs are ranked by how strongly the
maintenance of the construct obstructs growth. A construct that gives good
hydrolase activity but a large fitness cost is a poor candidate for a chassis
that has to persist in the gut, and the ranking is what allows that trade-off
to be made explicitly.

Growth is read as colony formation on solid medium rather than as optical
density in liquid. Spotting 5 µL of each of three consecutive dilutions onto
one Omnitray places every construct on the same plate, in the same agar, imaged
by the same camera, which removes the plate-to-plate and well-to-well variation
that otherwise dominates small differences in growth rate. The plate is
incubated and imaged continuously for 30 hours, so that both the final colony
count and the time at which colonies appear are available for the ranking.

The drop plate format used here is the one described by Herigstad et al.
(2001), in which a small volume of each dilution is deposited on the agar
surface instead of a full spread plate. It uses far less medium and far fewer
plates than spread plating, at the cost of a narrower countable range per spot,
which is why three consecutive dilutions are always plated.

#### Attributions

The drop plate format and the guidance on countable spots are taken from
Herigstad et al. (2001). The framing of plasmid maintenance as a measurable
fitness cost follows San Millan & MacLean (2017).

The imaging and incubation conditions are those of the Reshape Biotech imaging
incubator used in the laboratory (Reshape Biotech, n.d.).

#### Materials

**Strains**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| *E. coli* carrying each BSH construct | One cryostock per construct; eight constructs fit one Omnitray layout |
| *E. coli* carrying the empty backbone, and untransformed *E. coli* | The construct-free references against which the fitness cost is expressed |

**Reagents**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| LB broth | 5 mL per overnight culture and per subculture |
| LB agar with kanamycin | 20 mL per Omnitray |
| Kanamycin stock solution, 50 mg/mL | Final concentration 50 µg/mL in both broth and agar |
| LB broth for the dilution series | 90 µL per dilution step; sterile |
| Ethanol, 70% (v/v) | For disinfecting the bench and the plate surface |

**Consumables and tools**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Omnitray single-well plates | One per run; the plate the dilutions are spotted on |
| 96-well microtiter plates, sterile | One per run, for the dilution series |
| Culture tubes, 15 mL | One per strain for the overnight culture and one for the subculture |
| Cuvettes for OD₆₀₀ | One per measurement |
| Multichannel pipette, micropipettes and sterile tips | 0.5–10 µL, 20–200 µL and 100–1000 µL |
| Reservoirs | For the multichannel pipette |

**Instruments**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Shaking incubator | 37 °C, 200 rpm |
| Spectrophotometer | OD₆₀₀ |
| Imaging incubator (Reshape Biotech) | Timelapse imaging with incubation from 10 to 40 °C; takes Omnitray and microplate formats (Reshape Biotech, n.d.) |
| Autoclave | For sterilizing media and glassware |

**Software**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Reshape Biotech analysis software | Colony detection and counting from the timelapse images |

**Personal protective equipment**

| Item | Specification and quantity |
|---|---|
| Lab coat | Worn closed at all times |
| Nitrile gloves | Changed after contact with reagents or cultures |
| Safety glasses | Required when handling hot liquids and irritant powders |

#### Safety and safety data sheets

Hazard classifications were taken from the supplier safety data sheets (SDS)
and product pages cited in the table below. The SDS of the exact product and
lot in use should be consulted before starting, because classifications can
differ between suppliers and product grades.

The strains handled here are genetically modified micro-organisms and are
worked with under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Kanamycin
powder is weighed in a fume hood; the stock solution is handled with gloves.

**Table 1.** Hazard classification and safety data sheets (SDS) of the reagents used.

| Substance | CAS no. | Classification (GHS/CLP) | Precautions | SDS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kanamycin sulfate | 25389-94-0 | Danger. H360D: may damage the unborn child (Repr. 1B). | Wear gloves, lab coat and safety glasses. Weigh powder in a fume hood. Not to be handled during pregnancy. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-b) |
| Ethanol, 70% (v/v) | 64-17-5 | Danger. H225: highly flammable liquid and vapour. H319: causes serious eye irritation. | Keep away from open flames and hot surfaces. | Sigma-Aldrich (n.d.-a) SDS of absolute ethanol |
| Bacterial strains and transformants | n/a | Biological agents. Transformants are genetically modified micro-organisms (contained use). | Work under the containment level approved for the laboratory. Inactivate all cultures, plates and contaminated consumables by autoclaving. | No SDS: see the institutional biosafety guidelines |

**Waste disposal.** All cultures, plates and contaminated consumables are
inactivated by autoclaving before disposal. The waste and disposal instructions
of the laboratory in use apply.

<Callout title="Still to be added">

The exact model of the Reshape Biotech instrument, the catalogue number of the
Omnitray plates and the identity of the eight BSH constructs in rows A to H
still need to be added. The photograph of a spotted plate held by the team
would be a useful illustration here, with a caption identifying the strains,
the dilutions and the medium; it has to be uploaded through the iGEM uploads
tool first.

</Callout>

#### Procedure

**Day 0: overnight cultures**

1. Prepare 5 mL of LB broth with kanamycin per strain, and inoculate each from
   the corresponding cryostock.
2. Include the construct-free reference strains in the same way, in LB broth
   without kanamycin for the untransformed strain.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The runs reported in October 2026 were performed without a construct-free
control. Without it the fitness cost cannot be expressed as a reduction
relative to unburdened growth, only as a difference between constructs. Every
future run includes at least one construct-free reference strain.

</ProtocolNote>

3. Incubate overnight at 37 °C with shaking at 200 rpm.

<ProtocolNote>

All strains are started from cryostock rather than from a plate, so that none
of them has had a different number of generations of prior growth. A difference
in the starting state of the culture translates directly into an apparent
difference in fitness.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: subculture to exponential phase**

4. Dilute each overnight culture 1 in 50 into fresh LB broth with kanamycin and
   incubate at 37 °C with shaking at 200 rpm.
5. Measure the OD₆₀₀ of each subculture until it reaches or exceeds 0.5.

<ProtocolNote>

A 1 in 50 dilution reaches an OD₆₀₀ of approximately 0.5 in about 3 hours. A 1
in 100 dilution takes approximately 6 hours and is used when the subculture has
to be started early in the day.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

All strains are spotted from the same growth phase. Cells taken from stationary
phase form colonies more slowly than cells taken from exponential phase, and
that lag is indistinguishable from a fitness cost in the images.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: normalizing to OD₆₀₀ = 0.5**

6. Calculate, for each strain, the volume of subculture and the volume of fresh
   LB broth needed to reach an OD₆₀₀ of 0.5 (Table 2).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={2}
  title="Normalization to OD₆₀₀ = 0.5 (OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2)."
  workbookTab="Fitness ranking"
  rows={[
    { key: "od1", label: "Measured OD₆₀₀ of the subculture (OD1)", value: 0.84, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "od2", label: "Target OD₆₀₀ (OD2)", value: 0.5, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "v2", label: "Final volume of the normalized culture (V2)", value: 1000, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "v1", label: "Subculture to use (V1)", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.od2 * v.v2) / v.od1 },
    { key: "vBroth", label: "Fresh LB broth to add", unit: "µL", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.v2 - v.v1 },
    { key: "dense", label: "Is the subculture dense enough to normalize?", unit: "–", computeText: (v) => (v.od1 >= v.od2 ? "Yes — proceed" : "No — return it to the incubator") },
  ]}
/>

7. Prepare the normalized cultures in clean tubes and mix thoroughly.

<ProtocolNote>

The normalization is the step that makes the assay comparative. Every spot on
the plate then starts from the same cell density, so a difference in the number
or the size of the colonies reflects the construct and not the inoculum.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1: ten-fold dilution series**

8. Pipette 90 µL of sterile LB broth into the wells of a 96-well plate that
   will hold the dilutions, one row per strain.
9. Transfer 10 µL of the normalized culture into the first well, mix by
   pipetting, and carry 10 µL forward into each following well, changing tips
   at every step (Table 3).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={3}
  title="Ten-fold dilution series."
  workbookTab="Fitness ranking"
  rows={[
    { key: "vTransfer", label: "Volume transferred per step", value: 10, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "vDiluent", label: "Medium already in each well", value: 90, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "steps", label: "Number of steps performed", value: 7, unit: "steps", input: true },
    { key: "factor", label: "Dilution factor per step", unit: "–", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (v.vTransfer + v.vDiluent) / v.vTransfer },
    { key: "dilution", label: "Dilution of the culture after n steps", unit: "–", decimals: 10, compute: (v) => Math.pow(v.factor, -v.steps) },
    { key: "vWell", label: "Total volume per well before the transfer out", unit: "µL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.vTransfer + v.vDiluent },
  ]}
/>

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The tips are changed at every step. Carrying cells over on the outside of a tip
raises the apparent count of every dilution below it and flattens the series,
which is visible afterwards as a set of spots that do not differ tenfold from
one another.

</ProtocolNote>

10. Continue the series to 10⁻⁷, so that the 10⁻⁵, 10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁷ dilutions are
    available for spotting.

**Day 1: spotting the Omnitray**

11. Pour 20 mL of LB agar with kanamycin per Omnitray and allow the plates to
    set and dry.

<ProtocolNote>

Twenty millilitres gives a level surface at a depth that does not absorb the
spot too quickly. A plate that is still wet lets the spots run together; a
plate that is too dry absorbs them before they spread evenly.

</ProtocolNote>

12. Mark the plate with the layout to be used, so that every spot can be
    assigned to a strain, a replicate and a dilution (Table 4).

**Table 4.** Omnitray layout used, with eight strains in rows A to H, two
technical replicates and three dilutions per replicate. The cell gives the
label of the spot: the row letter followed by the replicate number.

| Row | 10⁻⁷ (rep. 2) | 10⁻⁶ (rep. 2) | 10⁻⁵ (rep. 2) | 10⁻⁷ (rep. 1) | 10⁻⁶ (rep. 1) | 10⁻⁵ (rep. 1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | A2 | A2 | A2 | A1 | A1 | A1 |
| B | B2 | B2 | B2 | B1 | B1 | B1 |
| C | C2 | C2 | C2 | C1 | C1 | C1 |
| D | D2 | D2 | D2 | D1 | D1 | D1 |
| E | E2 | E2 | E2 | E1 | E1 | E1 |
| F | F2 | F2 | F2 | F1 | F1 | F1 |
| G | G2 | G2 | G2 | G1 | G1 | G1 |
| H | H2 | H2 | H2 | H1 | H1 | H1 |

<ProtocolNote>

The layout places the two technical replicates of a strain on the same row, as
two blocks of three dilutions, with the dilutions running from the most dilute
on the outside to the least dilute towards the middle of each block. Row H is
reserved for the construct-free reference strain in future runs.

</ProtocolNote>

13. Spot 5 µL of each of the 10⁻⁵, 10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁷ dilutions onto its assigned
    position, keeping the pipette perpendicular to the agar and touching the
    drop to the surface without pressing into it.

<ProtocolNote>

Three consecutive dilutions are always spotted because the countable range of a
5 µL drop is narrow. At least one of the three normally falls in the range
where colonies are separate and can be counted (Herigstad et al., 2001).

</ProtocolNote>

14. Allow the spots to dry completely with the lid off, in the flow cabinet,
    before moving the plate.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

A plate moved before the spots have dried lets the drops run across the agar
and merge, and the run is lost. Drying takes longer on a freshly poured plate
than on one that has been stored.

</ProtocolNote>

**Day 1 to 2: incubation and imaging**

15. Load the plate into the imaging incubator and run for 30 hours at 37 °C,
    with the capture interval set so that the appearance of the first colonies
    is resolved.

<ProtocolNote>

The instrument used supports capture intervals from 10 minutes to 12 hours and
incubation from 10 to 40 °C, and takes the Omnitray format directly (Reshape
Biotech, n.d.).

</ProtocolNote>

16. Record the plate identifier, the layout and the run conditions with the
    image series.

**Counting and ranking**

17. Select, for each spot, the dilution at which the colonies are separate and
    countable, and count the colonies in that spot.

<ProtocolNote>

Spots in which the colonies have merged into a lawn are recorded as too
numerous to count and are not used. The next dilution along is used instead.

</ProtocolNote>

18. Calculate the colony-forming units per millilitre of the normalized culture
    from the count, the spot volume and the dilution factor (Table 5).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={5}
  title="Colony-forming units per millilitre from a spot."
  workbookTab="Fitness ranking"
  rows={[
    { key: "colonies", label: "Colonies counted in the spot", value: 24, unit: "CFU", input: true },
    { key: "vSpotUl", label: "Volume spotted", value: 5, unit: "µL", input: true },
    { key: "exponent", label: "Dilution spotted, as the exponent of 10⁻ˣ", value: 6, unit: "–", input: true },
    { key: "vSpotMl", label: "Volume spotted", unit: "mL", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => v.vSpotUl / 1000 },
    { key: "cfu", label: "CFU per mL of the normalized culture", unit: "CFU/mL", decimals: 0, compute: (v) => v.colonies / (v.vSpotMl * Math.pow(10, -v.exponent)) },
    { key: "countable", label: "Is the spot in the countable range?", unit: "–", computeText: (v) => (v.colonies >= 3 && v.colonies <= 30 ? "Yes — 3 to 30 colonies" : "No — use the next dilution along") },
  ]}
/>

19. Average the two technical replicates of each strain.
20. Express the result of each construct relative to the construct-free
    reference strain, as a relative colony-forming unit yield and as a relative
    fitness (Table 6).

<ProtocolCalculator
  number={6}
  title="Relative yield, fitness cost and delay."
  workbookTab="Fitness ranking"
  rows={[
    { key: "cConstruct", label: "CFU/mL of the strain carrying the construct", value: 4.8, unit: "× 10⁹ CFU/mL", input: true },
    { key: "cReference", label: "CFU/mL of the construct-free reference strain", value: 7.2, unit: "× 10⁹ CFU/mL", input: true },
    { key: "yield", label: "Relative CFU yield", unit: "–", decimals: 3, compute: (v) => v.cConstruct / v.cReference },
    { key: "cost", label: "Fitness cost of the construct", unit: "%", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => (1 - v.yield) * 100 },
    { key: "tConstruct", label: "Time to first detectable colonies, construct", value: 9.5, unit: "h", input: true },
    { key: "tReference", label: "Time to first detectable colonies, reference", value: 7, unit: "h", input: true },
    { key: "delay", label: "Delay in the appearance of colonies", unit: "h", decimals: 1, compute: (v) => v.tConstruct - v.tReference },
  ]}
/>

21. Read the time at which colonies first become detectable in each spot from
    the image series, and record it alongside the count.

<ProtocolNote>

The time to detection is the second readout of the assay. Two constructs can
reach the same final count while one of them takes several hours longer to get
there, and that delay is a fitness cost that the endpoint count alone does not
show.

</ProtocolNote>

22. Rank the constructs by relative fitness, from the lowest cost to the
    highest, and report the ranking together with the time to detection and the
    number of independent runs it is based on.

<ProtocolNote kind="critical">

The ranking is comparative and holds only within a run. Constructs are compared
with one another on the same plate, and runs are compared with one another only
through the construct-free reference they share.

</ProtocolNote>

#### Notes from the bench

This section collects the experience of the team with this protocol: what was
changed between runs, what went wrong and what is worth knowing before
starting. Members of the team are encouraged to add their own entries, each
starting with the month and year.

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The first runs used three dilutions (10⁻⁵, 10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁷) spotted as 5 µL on
LB agar with kanamycin in an Omnitray, from cultures normalized to an OD₆₀₀ of
0.5. The dilution steps were 10 µL into 90 µL of medium.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

A 1 in 50 subculture dilution reached the working optical density in
approximately 3 hours, while a 1 in 100 dilution took approximately 6 hours.
The 1 in 50 dilution is the practical choice when the plate is to be spotted
the same afternoon.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

No construct-free control was included in the first runs. This is the single
most important addition for the next run, because the fitness cost can only be
expressed relative to a strain that does not carry a construct.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolNote date="October 2026">

The RESHAPE runs were 30 hours at 37 °C. Whether 30 hours is long enough for
the slowest constructs to reach a countable spot has not yet been checked
against the image series.

</ProtocolNote>

<ProtocolCalculations>

This section explains the calculations used in this protocol. The symbols are
defined for each equation, and the worked example uses the values printed in
the corresponding table.

**Normalization to OD₆₀₀ = 0.5**

OD1 × V1 = OD2 × V2 → V1 = (OD2 × V2) / OD1

in which:

- OD1 = measured OD₆₀₀ of the subculture
- V1 = volume of subculture to use (µL)
- OD2 = target OD₆₀₀, being 0.5
- V2 = final volume of the normalized culture (µL)

The optical density is treated as proportional to the cell density over the
range used here, so the relation is the same dilution relation as
C1 × V1 = C2 × V2. The volume of broth to add is V2 − V1. The subculture must
be above the target density for the normalization to be possible; a culture
below OD₆₀₀ 0.5 is returned to the incubator rather than concentrated.

**Worked example.** For a subculture at OD₆₀₀ 0.84 and a final volume of
1000 µL: V1 = (0.5 × 1000) / 0.84 = 595.2 µL of subculture, made up with
1000 − 595.2 = 404.8 µL of fresh LB broth.

**Ten-fold dilution series**

*f* = (*V*transfer + *V*diluent) / *V*transfer  and  *d*n = *f*^(−*n*)

in which:

- *f* = dilution factor per step
- *V*transfer = volume carried into the next well (µL)
- *V*diluent = volume of medium already in that well (µL)
- *n* = number of steps performed
- *d*n = dilution of the culture after *n* steps

Transferring 10 µL into 90 µL gives a total of 100 µL and therefore a ten-fold
dilution at every step, so that the exponent counts the steps directly. Tips
are changed at every step, because cells carried on the outside of a tip raise
the count of every dilution below it.

**Worked example.** With 10 µL transferred into 90 µL: *f* = (10 + 90) / 10 =
10. After 7 steps the dilution is 10⁻⁷, and the three dilutions spotted (10⁻⁵,
10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁷) are the products of 5, 6 and 7 steps.

**Colony-forming units per millilitre from a spot**

*C* = *N* / (*V*spot × *d*n)

in which:

- *C* = colony-forming units per millilitre of the normalized culture (CFU/mL)
- *N* = number of colonies counted in the spot (CFU)
- *V*spot = volume spotted, in millilitres
- *d*n = dilution of the culture that was spotted, as a fraction

The spot volume is converted from microlitres to millilitres before use: 5 µL
is 0.005 mL. A drop plate spot is counted when it holds roughly 3 to 30
colonies; above that the colonies touch and the count is an underestimate; a
spot that has merged into a lawn is recorded as too numerous to count and the
next dilution along is used instead. Spotting three consecutive dilutions makes
it likely that at least one of them falls in the countable range (Herigstad et
al., 2001).

**Worked example.** For 24 colonies in the 10⁻⁶ spot of 5 µL: *C* =
24 / (0.005 × 10⁻⁶) = 4.80 × 10⁹ CFU/mL of the normalized culture.

**Relative yield, fitness cost and delay**

*Y* = *C*construct / *C*reference · cost = (1 − *Y*) × 100 · Δ*t* = *t*construct − *t*reference

in which:

- *Y* = colony-forming unit yield of the construct relative to the construct-free reference
- *C*construct = CFU/mL of the strain carrying the construct
- *C*reference = CFU/mL of the construct-free reference strain
- cost = fitness cost of the construct, as a percentage
- *t*construct = time at which colonies first become detectable for the construct (h)
- *t*reference = the same time for the construct-free reference (h)
- Δ*t* = delay in the appearance of colonies (h)

Both strains start from the same optical density, so a lower yield means that
fewer of the cells spotted went on to form a colony in the time available,
which is the fitness cost expressed as a count. The delay is reported alongside
it because two constructs can reach the same final count with several hours
between them. Both quantities are comparative and are only meaningful against a
reference on the same plate; the constructs are then ranked from the lowest
cost to the highest.

**Worked example.** For a construct at 4.80 × 10⁹ CFU/mL against a
construct-free reference at 7.20 × 10⁹ CFU/mL: *Y* =
4.80 × 10⁹ / 7.20 × 10⁹ = 0.667, so the cost is (1 − 0.667) × 100 = 33.3%.
With colonies first detected at 9.5 h for the construct and 7.0 h for the
reference, Δ*t* = 2.5 h.

</ProtocolCalculations>
